Shadows of Titan




SHADOWS OF TITAN








Ji LaRouche
________________










Prelude
General Helios King stood rigidly at the top of the Aegis Ascension Nexus’ grand citadel, his hot gaze sweeping over the city of Helion below. A green and blue sparkling labyrinth of shining towers and sprawling districts, alive in the light of the far-off sun. The air buzzed with the hum of anti-grav vehicles weaving between the vertical jungle of glass and titanium. Neon lights flickered in shades of blues and purples, illuminating the meticulously divided zones of Apex supremacy and Dreg despair. Not even Helion Prime's perfection could chase away the pooling of dread in King’s mind. The faint shimmer of his golden exosuit pulsed as his power subtly fluctuated under the intense strain of his emotions. Power rolled off of him in waves. Solar Resonance pulsed strong within him, a constant reminder of his dominance yet tonight it felt strained. Fragile. He tightened his grip on the railing, fighting the tremble in his hands.
“General.” Lynx Kairen’s voice came low and steady from behind him in the shadows, where he always seemed to linger. His Nullify mutation dampened the very air around him but his words always carried that strange calming weight. “I told you, there were no eyes on the blast. No one saw anything. Whatever happened... it’s done.”
King’s jaw tightened. “Her powers... They were just too much.” He clenched his eyes closed, pain twisting across his brow. Wishing he could forget the explosion, her almost Quasar-tier state, her body crumbling into fragments of time and space before his attack dissolved her into nothingness. He thought he had been careful. He was sure nothing could survive a blast like that. But something felt wrong. A gnawing unease. Like he had been watched.


***


Darius Valkyrie leaned back against the smooth metal under-ledge of the citadel’s roof, his Volcanic energy coiled beneath the surface of his skin as a soft sigh escaped his lips. His molten gaze fixed on Helios King’s solitary figure. He’d seen it all. But had been too scared to interfere, too scared to save her. Her existence burned in his mind like molten magma threatening to erupt. For now, he’d bide his time. Until he understood. Understood what actually happened.
The communicator on the General’s wrist blinked surprisingly bright against the light of his dimming Solar Resonance. The solar giant looked drained. Darius watched the older man's expression darken as he answered, his words hushed and deliberate.
“REPORT TO THE AAN COUNCIL CHAMBERS,” came the crackling distorted voice from the other end. “IMMEDIATELY.” The line crackled quiet.
King stood, squared his shoulders and disappeared in the yawning archway leading back into the depths of the AAN leaving Valkyrie alone with his thoughts and the night.
Helion Prime was a marvel of architecture and brutal ambition, carved from Titan’s unforgiving terrain. The Apex Sectors’ crystalline spires soared above the rest, shimmering like sharpened diamonds. Below a crystalline banner, the Luminal District buzzed with the kinetic motion of tower rails, vertical walkways, and the frantic pulse of its citizens clawing upward chasing status like it was oxygen. Deeper sat the Null Zone, a grimy grey existence that hummed with the static of miserable survival. And deeper still, the Undercity festered, its own cloaked existence thriving in the shadows of desperation, rust and decay. At the city’s heart was the Ascension Spire, the seat of the Aegis Ascension Nexus. From its crystalline perch the Apex Corps ruled with a titanium armoured hand, enforcing their doctrine of supremacy, law, order, and oppression. From Darius’s perch, the city looked exactly as it always did, strained and beautifully blind to its own rot. He belonged here at the top, sure. But even he could smell the rot beneath the throne.


***


The Apex Corps headquarters was alive with the kinetic fury of grappling cries, sparring grunts, and the crackle of power surging against containment fields. Darius moved through the halls, scorching heat rippling off him, salutes of greeting following in his wake. Some respectful, some wary, all very aware of his power. Towering over the average at six-foot-eight and a body built like a fortress, his fiery amber eyes only added to his aura of intimidation and confidence.
“Darius! You look like you’re about to melt someone again!” Eira Solaris called, stepping from the frost-edged arena with a smile that didn't quite reach her cold blue eyes. Around her, her silvery hair danced in flecks of frost, ice, and flame, a lingering echo of her Cryokinesis. Her fun demeanor veiling the storm she could unleash when provoked.
“Maybe I am,” Darius replied, his tone light but his Volcanic powers simmering beneath his skin, a constant temptation to erupt. “You’ve seen the Null reports. Illegal tech smuggling is at an all-time high and is completely out of control! Just slipping through our hands like lava and we’re too busy posturing to notice.”
“Collapse?” A voice behind him shimmered with light just like the man did, bright light fractals colliding together to make Seren Kaelith. His Starlight shimmer trailing like a rainbow of lazy fireflies. “Sounds dramatic, even for you. The Council has everything under control. They always do.”
“And just how exactly would you know that?” Darius shot back through gritted teeth. “You spend half your time polishing your face and the other half pretending to care about Luminals while not having to touch them.”
Seren’s grin was maddeningly serene. “We all have our roles to play Valkyrie. Mine just doesn’t involve getting dirt under my nails.”
“Alright enough you two.” Eira’s voice cut through the air like a frozen blade. She walked between them languidly, her boots leaving crystalline footprints to bloom in the silence. “How about you boys save this for after the briefing, hm?” she asked coolly, already walking toward the command centre, the air behind her sizzling where the heat met the ice.
The command centre of the Apex Corps was like the brain of a god, circular, and encased in polished obsidian glass. Glowing data floated in mid-air, displaying threat assessments and power level readouts in real time. From the central dais, General Helios King stood tall and proud, light filtering through the chamber’s high dome and catching the gold of his exosuit, causing it to glow.
“Reports,” he boomed. “Indicate a spike in illegal tech circulation across the Luminal District. Adaptive Implants.” He turned, pacing. “Surge amplifiers. Bootleg exo-shells.” His gaze swept the room. “Where is our presence? Our visibility? Our control?” King’s eyes settled briefly on Darius before continuing the sweep.
Darius stood at parade rest beside Eira and Seren, jaw locked. He said nothing about what he had seen but he could feel it gnawing at his mind. Some control.
“We’re increasing assignments to three-unit patrols,” King continued. “You’ll report any unrest, suppress it any way needed, trace any illegal or black market movement back to its source and SNUFF. IT. OUT.” Veins of light crept up his neck but then retreated. “Quietly. Understood?” he finished, turning to face the group.
Darius nodded once. “General.” Was all he said.
Seren’s chipper voice pierced the tension, “Any priority targets?” he asked.
“Anyone trying to hide.” King’s words were pointed. “Dismissed.”
The team exited the chamber and as the doors closed the golden light behind them finally dimmed. Darius let out a breath he hadn’t realised he had been holding; King’s lack of control bothered him deeply. Under normal circumstances seeing the old man come unglued would have been amazing, but in light of recent events...
The transport hummed beneath his boots, hovering above the Luminal District like a blade waiting to drop. Below them the city throbbed with restless energy, bustling markets, neon tech stalls, and sputtering old world style factories. Holographic banners flared displaying Apex slogans like, “Rise Through Obedience!” or “Only Those Who Adapt Survive!”
“The Lumi’s are starting to get desperate,” Eira murmured, her eyes scanning the streets below.
“Oh come on, can you blame them?” Seren said flippantly as he shifted from one foot to the other. “They’re stuck in a caste system. I’d be a little desperate too.”
Darius crossed his arms as he watched the side alleyways. “Keep talking like that and someone’s going to drop you into a caste system as well, Kaelith.”
“Someone already tried,” Seren responded lightly even though his expression darkened.
“Enough.” Eira cut in, her tone flat. “We’re not here to philosophise.” She tapped her communicator. “Unit Nine, Sector C Sweep initiated.” The team leapt out of the transport and descended gracefully, boots hitting the concrete with practiced synchronisation. Eyes immediately turned and people began to swarm immediately; their work had begun.
________________






















Chapter 1
She was drifting through oblivion. There was no up or down, just formless black that pulsed like an invisible current around her. Her very existence floated through the void like it was being pulled and unraveled, rewoven into the breathless dark. 
She was... dying?
She buzzed with the silence, like the entire universe was humming just out of reach. A flicker of memory lanced through her, causing her to try to cry out. Helios King’s cruel gaze looking down on her, hot as star-fire, mouth tight with focus. A pillar of pure white light descended behind him, searing the sky above. She remembered the crack, the impossible noise. The moment the air fractured into blinding light. Her own scream began ringing painfully in her ears as her flesh was being eaten by searing light, the pain of betrayal that lingered the strongest. Her mouth hung open but she couldn’t draw a breath, couldn't make a sound. She didn’t want to go out like this! Whatever this is? This isn't the end, right?
“Focus.”
The word cracked through the dark, warped and distant, like thunder underwater. She could feel the last bits of energy flickering faintly in her as she drew on her last bit of willpower, clawing at the edges of existence. Anything to hold her together and to pull her back from fading into nothingness.
Pull it together. She whispered through gritted teeth. She was not about to go out like this. Dragging every shred of consciousness she could back into form, she felt a surge ripple towards her. She flung herself at it with all her might and suddenly the void shattered, exploding like hot glass as she fell through with a sickening crash.
Myth slammed into the ground, hard enough to crack the breath back into her lungs with a gasping painful shudder. Her vision fractured and for a second all she could see was static. She lay still where she fell, letting the foul and metallic air scrape down her throat as she choked on the Undercity’s rot. Lights flickered dimly above her, artificial and jittering like they didn’t have near the energy reserves to burn steadily. Towering metal pressed in on her from all sides, jagged and twisted, silhouetted against the methane glow seeping down from the surface fissures. The stench hit her nose next. Something had died long ago nearby but from the smell it might not be dead enough.
Myth rolled onto her side with a groan. Her body ached and was slick with sweat. She pushed herself to her hands and knees and tried to cover herself with her hands. Her dark skin shimmered faintly where the dim light caught it and she was cold and thrumming from exhaustion.
Her mind came in shards back to back in flashes of fragmented colours. Pain, light, King, heat, silence. She couldn’t hold onto them and they crumbled like ash into nothing. Then they were gone.
“Where... am I?” Her voice was hoarse and unfamiliar, her throat dry and cracked. She staggered to her feet, her legs buckling once before catching. She moved gingerly down the alleyway stooping here and there grabbing at errant cloths and trash until she found enough discarded rags enough to cover her body, the important parts at least. A discarded boot here and there until she found some that somewhat stayed to her feet. As she made her way to the entrance she could see Dregs limping past like ghosts in the smog, their bodies warped by mutation and misery. Nobody even glanced her way; that was the way it was down here. An embedded clock on a stall wall read: Day 238 of Cycle Year 295.
“Day 238,” she whispered to herself, her voice dry. “At least someone’s keeping track.” She leaned against the alley wall breathing tightly through her teeth. Her finger twitched and her stomach turned, then twisted. Hunger hit like a wave of fire tearing through her veins. Myth doubled over, arms wrapped tight around herself, her breath now coming faster and jagged.
No. Not yet. She hissed in pain as the ache sharpened. Her body staggered forward the edge of a rusted crate catching onto her. She was barely aware of what she was doing until her palm met the metal. Her fingers tingled and a tiny spark jumped between them as the crate began to dissolve, silently and beautifully into drifting threads of molten silver that absorbed into her skin like mist pulled into a vacuum. The power pulsed briefly as it faded into her body.
Not enough. Not even close.
It was like licking condensation off a glass, quenching and cooling for a second but then the thirst returns deeper and drier than before. Myth exhaled shakily and stepped back from the vanishing edge of the crate letting her hand fall back to her side. She knew better than this. But inanimate things were safe. Unquestioning. Unfeeling. But they felt like old ash on a fire. Just enough to leave a flicker to keep her barely moving.
She needed living energy… Life.
The thought made her flinch. Her body had tasted it before... she knew it. Tasted its warmth, its fullness. She could almost taste the way it curled inside her, electric and pulsing.
No.
Myth’s eyes swept the broken street, trying to figure out her next step. Staying here exposed was a mistake and dangerous. She cinched her rags tighter, folding herself into them. With careful steps she followed her nose towards an alleyway with the smell of smoke. Acrid. Familiar. She turned toward it, careful not to draw attention, following the bitter trail to a side backstreet nearly swallowed by the wet dark. There, tucked into a pocket of decay a weak fire crackled in its recesses, its glow struggling against the wet dark. Around it huddled half a dozen or more twisted silhouettes of people. Arms too long with eyes that held a faint burning yellow glow. Dregs.
She hesitated. Trust was thinner than the air down here but time was running out. Her energy reserves were plummeting, and the gnawing ache in her gut was ready to sharpen again. Steeling herself, Myth stepped into the light. Her eyes caught the glow of the fire and flared faintly. “I need help,” she said. Her voice was quiet but steady. She forced the tremor in her limbs not to show.
The Dregs turned slowly and stared at her. One of them, a wiry man with a face like folded leather, tilted his head towards her. The firelight turned his eyes to molten gold as he studied her intently. “You don’t look like you belong here,” he snarled slowly, voice caught between suspicion and curiosity. “Who are you?”
Myth stared at the man, her jaw tightening. She wasn’t about to give him more information than he needed.
“I’m just someone who's trying not to die,” she replied truthfully.
The wiry man tilted his head, the bones in his neck popping as he slouched toward her.
“Aren't we all?” He laughed crudely. “There’s someone who might could use a body though. Vexal Ophedius. He’s got a little enclave. Space. Protection. Even… connections. But nothing’s free down here.” He smiled at her slowly, looking her scantily clad body up and down.
“Take me there,” she said flatly, her stomach twisting into an even tighter knot.
“Sure, sure,” he purred. He stepped closer, his fingers brushing her cheek. “Let’s get you settled, pretty thing.”
She turned before he could touch her again and started walking briskly in what she was hoping was the right direction. She heard him let out a low chuckle and quickly shuffle to overtake her, not bothering to say anything to his silent counterparts that hadn’t even attempted to join the conversation. As they made their way back into the street the city sounds whispered around them; wet boots on the broken and rotting metal, whispered trades in half-lit corners, generators pulsing like struggling heartbeats.
“Are we close?” Myth asked, her voice low, her eyes fixated on the back of the man's shaggy head..
He grinned over his shoulder. “One more right turn,” he rasped in a sing-song voice. “Then one left turn. Then you’re at the Big O baby! But first...” He spun suddenly, blocking her path. His hands reached for her hips, grimy fingers twitching with barely contained excitement. “How ‘bout a little something for the road?”
She didn’t think. The walk had made her ravenous and her hand shot forward, fingers grazing his chest. The reaction was instant.
His body spasmed, a gurgling sound trapped in his throat as his flesh began to unravel like threads of radiant dust. He was gone with his scream never fully formed.
Myth staggered back, her chest heaving. The energy surged through her, sharp, sweet, and raw. Her skin glowed faintly, her thoughts becoming clearer now. The ache finally left her limbs and she stood tall and straight, wiping her mouth. I had to. She whimpered. Guilt tried to coil in her gut but she shook it off; it was either him or her. And she much rather it be him. Myth hurried on following the man's last instruction and hoped he wasn’t lying as the street narrowed while she moved deeper into the Undercity.
Myth finally found the right corridor and followed the twisted metal pipes and half-dead lights until she stopped before a rusted door marked by a large, faded “O” painted in something far darker and redder than normal paint.
She squinted and scanned the shadows on both sides of the decrepit building but nothing stirred. She finally knocked on the door sharply. A voice rasped from inside, it sounded rough like he had been chewing gravel. “It’s open. Don’t waste my time.”
Myth pushed the door inward slowly. The dilapidated room beyond was dimly lit and strewn with pieces of recycled tech, heat lamps, and cybernetic parts twitching like insects on various tables and run-down couches. It smelled strongly of solder, blood and something else she couldn’t quite place. The hum of unstable wiring whistled occasionally in her ears and at the centre of the room, hunched over a flickering array of tech, sat Vexal Ophedius. The large red O patch on his shoulder and air of authority pulsed off him in intoxicating waves.
He didn’t rise, look up, or even blink. The glow of a side monitor lit the ruin of his half-reconstructed face, half exposed under a heavy dark hood. Cables snaked from where he was working into the walls behind him, pulsing faintly like veins on life support.
Myth stepped inside, mentally steeling herself. “My name is... Myth. I need food. And shelter.”
Vexal’s head tilted slowly. “And what’s that got to do with me?”
Myth took a step closer letting the door swing behind her. “I heard you have resources and needed bodies. I have usefulness.”
“That’s vague,” he said. “I don’t like vague.”
“I can run. Fight. Keep my mouth shut.”
At that, Vexal turned abruptly. His eyes were a stark stormy grey, but something behind them was very much alive. “You’re not from here, are you?”
“No,” she replied shortly. “And I don’t remember where I’m from.”
“That’s a dangerous answer,” he murmured deeply, leaning back and crossing his surprisingly muscular arms. A long silence stretched between them, giving her an opportunity to really look at Vexal. He wore a tattered black cloak, a mess of cloth and wires cinched with a length of frayed cord that looked like it had once belonged to something industrial. His pants were patched with mismatched patches, stitched over so many times the original pant colour was indistinguishable from the patches. One of his boots was rigged with some kind of support tech and  humming faintly; the other looked like it might collapse right off his foot if not for the metal band clamped to his shin.
His arms, though, kept drawing her eyes back. Muscle roped with scars and scorched circuit paths, crawling with pulses of black energy that vanished beneath the fringes of his cloak. His skin was so pale it almost seemed to glow. He turned his face up just enough for the light to catch his features. She almost gasped. The left side of Vexal’s face had been torn apart at some point in his life—torn, burned, or worse. In its place was an intricate lattice of reinforced synthetic bone, the kind used in exo-repair but cruder. Wires spiderwebbed out from a metal plate grated into his temple, pulsing with sluggish light. His cheek had been replaced entirely by a patchwork of dark mesh and plated tubing, a filter embedded near his jaw hissing softly with each breath.
But the other half was so handsome it was almost painful in contrast. The unscarred part of his face was pale and unblemished, sharp and hollowed slightly from malnutrition. His hair was inky black, thick at the crown and long. And his eyes were both beautiful cold, pale grey pools like ash in a long-dead fire, watching her as intently as she had been watching him.
He cleared his throat. “I could use a runner,” Vexal said, his voice flat. “Someone quiet and fast. Disposable if need be.” He arched an eyebrow at her.
Myth didn’t flinch. “That's fine.”
He nodded once, barely any motion at all. “There’s grub in the kitchen. Scrounge for what you need tonight. After that, you feed and fend for yourself. Don’t touch my stuff. And don’t speak to me unless it's important or worth dying over.”
Myth nodded her head in the beat of silence that followed, and their agreement settled between them like dust. Myth walked steadily out of the main room in the direction his chin had indicated and into what she assumed was the kitchen, not taking her eyes off of him until she was out of his line of sight.


The kitchen was a devastated skeleton with rusted metal counters and cabinets barely on their hinges with warped drawers hanging askew. The stink of decay and burnt oil made her nose sting painfully and her eyes hastily scanned looking for anything she could digest. A few discarded dishes sat in a pile near the sink, cracked and half-melted together from something overheating. She laid a hand on them; nobody would miss them and it would buy her more time to look for something of actual substance. They dissolved quickly like a light breath, not much except to slightly put off the gnawing in her core.
She sighed and turned to find Vexal was already there, leaning against the doorway watching her. “Interesting,” he said softly. “What exactly is your mutation classification, Myth?”
Myth met his gaze. For a long moment, she didn’t say anything. The words stuck in her throat, she knew how important what she said next was. Vexal didn't look away; he didn’t even blink. He just watched her, the quiet hiss of his jaw filter rising and falling like a slow, mechanical breath.
“I don’t really know yet,” she finally admitted. Her voice was low, edged with fatigue but steady. “I just know... I have to absorb things. Like eating. But not with my stomach. It’s more like...”
She hesitated again. Her hands curled slightly at her side. “Like I need to do it to stay together. Stay alive.”
The words hung between them. The flickering light from the broken fixture overhead cast a strange glow across her face, the silver in her eyes catching like storm-light in the dirty window behind Vexal. She wasn’t sure the answer he was looking for, but she had told the truth. 
Vexal’s head tilted, just enough for the overhead light to catch on the edges of his synthetic cheek-plate, casting fractured shadows across the floor.
“Absorb something then,” he said, almost casually. “Anything.”
He gestured with a faint click of his hand toward the cabinets, toward the decayed scraps of metal and melted dishware sitting long forgotten. Myth hesitated, then reached out. First the shelves dissolved instantly, threads of silver dust pulling into her skin, disappearing beneath the surface like rain into dry soil. Then the rest of the cabinets unraveled, the reaction was clean and controlled. 
She drew her hand back, flexing her fingers once. “Not really doing much,” she admitted quietly. She felt Vexal’s gaze sharpened. She hesitated again, then added. “I had... someone. On the way here. Just one, I swear!”
His interest flickered like a pulse through the wires in his temple. He straightened, the full height of him unsettling and engulfing the confined kitchen space. “Someone?” he echoed. “Alive?”
Myth didn’t answer, not directly. She didn’t have to; her face was enough. Vexal’s lips curled slightly, not quite a smile. Something darker. He opened his mouth to say something; she could feel it coming. A challenge, a test, something meant to push her. But before the words could leave him, the outer door slammed open with a sharp metallic clang.
“Sector sweeps!” a voice called from the hall. A tall lean woman with a sharp purple hair cut appeared in the doorway, out of breath and tense, her shadow buzzing about her heels. Her eyes were an icy lilac and were already scanning Vexal and the room, sensing something was out of place. “They’re hitting Null routes early. T.E.B. Squads are routing east side already. If you don’t move––”
“I know.” Vexal cut her off flatly. He didn’t look at Myth again. “Lyra Solvyn. Meet our new recruit, Myth.” He moved past her toward the hall. “She’s running with us tomorrow.” And just like that, he was gone.
The door snapped shut behind him with a hiss and a heavy click, leaving only the light hum of fading tech in his wake. Lyra stared at Myth for a long moment, her face unreadable. Then she exhaled and gave a dry, humourless smile.
“Well,” she said. “Welcome to the Big O.”
“Thanks,” Myth said, her voice still slightly rough but she was sincere. Lyra gave her a short nod, then turned without another word and led her deeper into the guts of the Big O.
They passed through a low hallway crammed with wires dangling like veins and greyish-blue ceiling panels that flickered weakly overhead. The further in they walked, the more people Myth began to see. Dregs mostly, their bodies twisted by unfavourable mutations, tech, or both. A red-headed woman with metal tubes instead of arms sat cross-legged in a crusty corner whispering to herself loudly. A tow-headed child with fractured green crystal growths around their eyes watched Myth as she walked by with unblinking intensity. Nobody spoke to her. Nobody asked any questions.
Lyra finally stopped at a narrow door wedged between two crates stacked high with coiled cables and stained blankets. She pushed it open with her boot.
“It’s not much,” she said. “But it locks.”
The room was barely more than a cubbyhole. A crate stood in for a bed, stacked with a few pieces of foam and what might have once been a blanket. There was a broken vent in the corner hissing faint warmth to keep the room from freezing. A stack of clothes sat on top of a pair of boots, both warming over the vent.
Lyra stepped back down the hallway. “Rest up,” she said over her shoulder. “You’re up at o-eight-hundred. If you’re late, Vexal won’t wait.”
Myth nodded once but Lyra had already disappeared down the corridor, and the moment the door clicked shut, silence returned like a cloak around her. Myth turned and slowly sank down, the crate creaking beneath her as she lay back, stretching out. The light leaking through the cracks was a sickly orange, constant and foggy. It was impossible to know how long the day's cycle had already run.
She stared at the ceiling for what felt like ages. Her body aching, still hungry beneath the surface. But that wasn’t what kept her awake. It was the blankness. She couldn’t remember anything past the alley. No faces. No names. No memories. She wasn’t even sure of her own name anymore. Just burning light, then nothing. She turned on her side, pulling the thin blanket tighter around her. Eventually, her breath slowed and the ache dulled. Her body sank into the crate and the hum of the Big O wrapped around her like static. And finally, Myth slept.




















Chapter 2
Myth’s breath ripped from her throat as she bolted upright, her chest heaving like she was still inside the nightmare. The light. Burning white and endless, pressing in from all sides, crawling under skin like a fire melting her bones. She could still feel it, searing and righteous. Then, nothing.
Another sharp knock on the door dragged her fully awake. She blinked hard, heart pounding, the dim orange glow of the overhead light and the cracked ceiling quickly coming into view. She scrambled to her feet, tugging the thin blanket tighter around her body, and opened the door just enough to peek out.
“Hour eight in ten,” Lyra said, already walking down the hallway. “Let’s go.”
Myth didn’t ask questions. She tossed her blanket and quickly dressed in the mismatched clothes. Slipping the oversized boots on, she closed the door tightly behind her before hurrying through the maze back to the front room. The main area of the Big O was buzzing now, people moving between stations, quiet voices trading information, materials, and whispered secrets. The same Dregs from the night before were here, but they seemed more alive now, less like ghosts.
Vexal was nowhere to be seen. Instead, standing near the front loading platform was a man built like a walking mountain. Broad-shouldered and tall, maybe six-foot-five, with arms like structural beams and a crooked grin that didn’t quite match the scar running down one cheek. His skin was a warm brown tone, marked by jagged lines that pulsed faintly with a dull Seismic glow.
“This is Korric Vale,” Lyra said, motioning toward him with a jerk of her chin. “You’ll be running with us today.”
Korric gave her a grin. “You the one Vexal dragged in last night?” His voice was deep, but kind. “Folks call me Break.”
“Myth,” she said, simply smiling at him a little.
“Course it is,” he said with a soft chuckle, grabbing a satchel from the nearby hook. “Let’s get moving. Today’s sweep takes us damn near Nocturne. Should be fun.”


***


They moved fast through the lower tunnels of the Undercity, Myth keeping close to Lyra as they weaved through the rust-choked corridors, half-collapsed access shafts, and glowing moss that flared faintly in the corners of shadowed alcoves. Pipes lined every surface overhead like vines, dripping condensation into oily puddles beneath their boots. She didn’t ask questions, and they didn’t talk. 
The Undercity wasn’t just one city down here, it was all of them. Cracked layers of buried zones, some forgotten, some overrun, stitched together by tunnels and routes the surface elite had long stopped mapping.
Their target ended up being a derelict node near the lower outskirts of Nocturne. When they got there, it looked like nothing. Just a collapsed shopfront half-swallowed by shadow and frost-damp stone.
Korric stood guard near the broken entryway while Lyra approached what looked like a sealed vendor terminal. She smiled at the cracked glass like a friend and started talking. Her tone changed, the way she stood changed. Myth blinked, startled by how effortlessly Lyra’s voice became soft, feminine, almost meek. Her stance slouched just enough, her fingers trembled like she was some broken thing. She was lying. Flawlessly.
A narrow slot opened on the side of the ruined terminal and Lyra reached in, pulled out a narrow package wrapped in twine, tucking it neatly in her coat. She straightened, turned, and was Lyra once again. Sharp, cold, and composed.
“Come on,” she commanded, and they were off again.
Korric grinned on and off the whole way back. “That was art,” he said finally as they walked. “You should teach the Dregs down in Riftspine that trick. They just throw rocks when they don’t get their way.”
Lyra rolled her eyes, but Myth could see the corner of her mouth twitch like she was hiding a smile.


***


By the time they returned to the Big O, something had shifted. The silence between them was no longer strained. Myth found herself walking alongside Korric and Lyra easily, her body moving in time with theirs. Lyra didn’t glare as hard and Korric didn’t tease quite as loudly. They weren’t friends yet, but the beginning of something had settled into the space between them. As they stepped inside, the buzz of generators and low conversation greeted them like a warm sigh.
Vexal was waiting. He stood at his workbench as if he’d never left, hunched over a web of sparking wires and stripped tech plating. His hood was down, his striking face fully exposed in the flicker of his cracked desk lamp.
Lyra didn’t say anything, walked over, and pulled the small package from her coat, sitting it down besides his elbow.
“Node was still operational,” she said. “Security was dormant.”
Vexal grunted. “About time.”
He didn’t look at her. Didn’t look at any of them. His hands were already prying open the parcel, revealing a handful of faded circuit tiles and a bent regulator coil. Nothing special that Myth could see. Just salvage. He went back to work without another word to them.
“Come on,” Korric murmured to Myth, jerking his head toward a hallway she hadn’t noticed before. “Let’s get you something better than those rags.”
The supply closet was a mess of repurposed uniforms, boots with mismatched soles, and makeshift armour rigs strung together with wire and faith. Korric rummaged through a few bins, tossing aside useless scraps until he found a pair of reinforced boots that only looked half-abused.
“Try these,” he said, handing them over. “They’re ugly, but they’ll keep your feet warm.”
He found her a pair of dark grey pants that mostly fit and a threadbare black long-sleeved tunic layered with some sort of polymer mesh. She changed in the corner while he respectfully turned his back and made a point of commenting on the closet's complete lack of organisation. When they emerged again, she felt relieved and not as exposed as she had been and disposed of her thread bare clothes in a nearby bin.


***


Evening hit the Undercity with no warning. The lights dimmed as power rerouted through the lower sectors. The mess area of the Big O flickered to life with soft chatter and the rattle of scavenged metal bowls.
They ate, if you could call it that, nutri-paste scraped from salvage tubes. It was threaded with odd chunks of plant protein, and thin broth recycled from fungal stock. The conversation around her was a lively blur. She barely heard when Lyra cracked a joke about someone mistaking rat meat for synth ham again. Korric laughed so hard it felt like the building was going to come down. Even Vexal offered a dry comment here or there from his seat at the back.
Myth tried to eat. She really did. She mimicked them, lifting the paste to her lips, chewing through the dense protein bits, even swallowed. But it was like... nothing. It passed through her like air. No weight, no warmth, no substance. Her stomach twisted tighter with each bite, hungrier and hungrier. Every swallow leaving her emptier than the last. She sat in silence trying to think. The others didn’t seem to notice, to her relief.
They were loud, warm, and real. Lyra teasing Korric about his second bowl. Korric trying to convince one of the younger Dregs that he’d once suplexed a mecha in Cryothorne. Vexal grunted occasionally in collaboration, only half-listening while still elbow-deep in glowing cables.
Myth smiled at the right times and tried to relax into the warmth of it all. Yet inside, something gnawed at her with spiked, relentless teeth.
By the time the others had trickled off to their rooms, Lyra mumbling something about reports and Korric yawning and saluting no one in particular, Myth found she could barely stand. Her body felt hollow, light, and brittle. Her breath came in tight little pulls like she was breathing in dry smoke.
She was starving for energy. She could feel it coiling deep in her chest like a second clawing heartbeat. It would be so easy to take it. Just one brush of skin, one breath, one pulse. She clenched her jaw, curling her hands into fists, and leaned forward until her forehead touched her knees. She needed to figure out an alternative fast but her brain was fuzzy and sluggish.
Vexal stood abruptly without a word. In one fluid and decisive movement, he grabbed his coat from the chair beside him, slinging it over his shoulders with a practiced jerk. His eyes cut toward her, unreadable in the dim light. Then, without a word, he crossed the room, gripped her arm, and hauled her gently to her feet.
“We’re going,” he said simply.
She didn’t protest, deciding to save her strength. Her legs moved beneath her barely keeping up; the urgency in his stride told her this wasn’t just a walk.


***


They moved through the Undercity like shadows, Vexal guiding her weakening body deeper and deeper into its recesses. Even the brave Dregs didn’t venture out this far at night, and certainly not without reason. By the time they reached an archway laced with pipe and synthetics that wasn’t crumbling, Myth could barely keep her head up.
Vexal stopped and looked her over. “Tell me. Exactly. What do you need?” he said, his voice low, eyes serious.
Myth licked her dry lips. Her vision swam, spots blooming at the edges. She was too far gone to even think of a lie.
“I need... energy,” she murmured. “Real energy. Not food or scraps. Not dishes. Not the rot off old Dregs. I need... something alive.”
Vexal didn’t blink or flinch. He didn’t speak. Instead, he turned on his heel and led her around the bend to what looked like the remains of a looming old holding block. It wasn’t abandoned, but the security system blinked in loops and looked like it hadn’t worked in more than a few cycle years.
Two enforcers stood posted outside. Not high-grade, but not entirely throwaways either. Clean gear and a practiced stance showed they were serious.
As they crossed the street, Vexal turned to her fully. The neon light of the Undercity caught just enough of his face, a reminder of how beautiful he was.
“You need me to take them out first?” he asked, deadly calm.
Myth shook her head. “No,” she gulped. “I can do it.”
She took one step forward. And another. They never saw it coming. The air shifted and thickened. Vibrating with tension. By the time the enforcers had registered her as a threat, a shimmer flashed from Myth’s chest and their bodies disintegrated into light dust. She hadn’t even touched them.
Their energy slammed into her like a shockwave. Her spine arched involuntarily as she felt it flood her cells like fire. Like the world turned inside out, she could see stars as her knees almost buckled. She was alive again.
Vexal stepped up beside her, silent, eyes wide with something she couldn’t place. He keyed the door open. Inside were two more enforcers and six prisoners hunkered in cells behind them. The moment Myth crossed the threshold, they all turned to look at her.
None of them got the chance to scream. They came apart under her hungry focus, shredding into glowing particles that flew toward her body like gravity. When it was done, her body thrummed with violent satisfaction. She could feel the wild grin across her face, her skin glowed faintly, the ache gone. But instead, it felt like something else had taken hold. She turned around, ready to face the horror she expected in Vexal’s expression.
But he was smiling. “You’re incredible,” he said, quiet and awed. “Do you feel better?”
She nodded, breathless and relieved. “I feel amazing!” she said, laughing. “Want to see what I can do with it?” she asked impulsively.
His face lit up like she’d offered him free credits. “Yeah,” he said. “Show me.”
She focused, pulling her hands together. The air around her sparked and suddenly, the prisoners and enforcers were back. Not real, not truly. But they looked real. They moved like echoes of their former selves, suspended in time and completely under her control. One took a step forward at her command; another raised its hand. Their skin was perfect, their limbs responsive.
Vexal approached one and poked it. No reaction. He reared back and punched it in the chest. The illusion held. He turned and looked at her in a way that made the buzzing feeling in her head flash through her entire body. She smiled and called them back. They dissolved instantly, their energy absorbed into her skin in graceful arcs of light. He was still staring at her. “How’d you feel?” he asked warily.
She met his eyes. “Amazing.”
He smirked down at her. “Then come on. Let’s go for a walk.” He turned and strode out to the street, checking behind his shoulder to make sure she was following. They walked for what felt like forever side by side. Quiet and peaceful. She tried not to think about what it meant, that he didn’t flinch away from her. That he was excited about what she could do.


***


They exited through an old maintenance tunnel, surprisingly well intact, and emerged onto the edge of a Forgedeep shopping nexus. The contrast hit like a slap.
Here the city was beautiful. The dome overhead reflected a diluted starlight and clean floodlamps bathed the plaza in synthetic day. She could see distant towers from other cities glittering like jewels in the distance. The people here were well-fed, well-dressed, and unconcerned about the desperate life crawling just beneath their feet. A blend of mid-tier Luminals and upper Dreg elites sipping cider and laughing without a care in the world.
Myth stared, her jaw agape.
Then the jealousy hit like bile. Vexal made some offhand comment she didn't quite hear, teasing, but it twisted in her chest. Before she knew what she was doing, her fingers moved. Something tore out of her quietly. An enormous mass, a beast made of sludge, acid, and violet energy. It hit the first person before anyone could react. Their scream was short, but their death was not. Myth gasped as the energy surged into her like a molten tide. Vexal stood beside her silently. He watched as her monster rampaged, the crowd fleeing in every direction. When the Apex alerts started to crackle from the comm lines overhead, he simply said, “Let’s go.”
They vanished back into the Undercity before the drones could arrive. She didn’t speak but she could tell he was happy. She could feel it. In the way he walked a little taller, a little closer. The way his eyes flicked toward her like she was something rare.
But there was a gnawing guilt at the back of her mind. Every surge of energy she felt from her monster came with a feeling of terror, pain. Like it was residuals of who it came from. She buried it in the back of her mind, refusing to dwell on it.
When they returned to the Big O, Vexal didn’t speak. He just peeled off his coat, rolled up his sleeves, and went straight to his workbench. She hovered nearby, unsure of what to do. He passed her a tool and she took it. They worked in near silence. Just the hiss of solder, the click of gear teeth, the faint pulse of power cells being slotted into place.
And when the first lines of dawn-light cracked the surface high above Titan’s skin, they were still there. Side by side.
























Chapter 3
The ForgeDeep sky was flickering in red and orange pulse warnings when the call came in. Darius Valkyrie felt the buzz before the alert even finished speaking.
“UNCONTAINED BIO-THREAT DETECTED. FORGEDEEP SHOPPING NEXUS 02Z09. PRIORITY ESCALATION: CODE NOVA. REPEAT. CODE NOVA.”
He turned sharply toward the others. Eira Solaris was already halfway through syncing her pacers, Solar fire crackling low around her palms, her eyes narrowing at the alert. Seren Kaelith’s expression was tighter than usual, his glow dimmed under the tension.
“They’re saying it’s... absorbing people?” Eira asked quietly, her face scrunched into a deep frown.


“Maybe a Dreg’s mutation spun out again?” Seren offered, but his voice lacked its usual teasing lilt. They didn’t waste time; the transport was already coasting high above the city spires, slicing through the heat and haze. From up here, the ForgeDeep shopping nexus looked like a burst wound, a jagged crater of ruin in what had been one of the more stable districts. The glitter of shattered glass and writhing movement below showed the thing was already far too close to the civilian quarters.
No time to think or plan; they jumped. The team landed hard and fast, boots cracking against the fractured plaza tile. Flames licked at the edges of the stalls, a shrill scream echoed somewhere behind a collapsed tram.
They saw it clearly then. A rolling mass of pulsing, acidic energy. Purple, black, and sick with movement. It was devouring and feeding on anything and anyone. A pair of shopkeepers staggered back slowly and were gone before they could even scream, their bodies unravelling like silk thread into the monster’s writhing mist.
Eira surged forward, hands blazing with frost fire, then ice. Her movements were snappy, sharp and precise, honed from years of perfect rhythm.
Nothing.
It didn’t even flinch. Darius joined her, the ground around him splitting with molten Magma heat. They moved in tandem, flame to frost building up the pressure and heat. Combos they had learned and perfected long ago. Still nothing.
The creature coiled and struck, faster than it looked like it could possibly move. Seren darted in a radiant pulse meant to blind it, but the monster surged toward him in a stream of liquid violence and hit him hard with a sickening crunch.
Seren was flung backward, crashing through a metal column entirely with a snap that echoed across the broken nexus.
“Seren!” Eira screamed, sliding across the ground towards him. She formed a cradle of ice mid-air, catching his unconscious body before it could slam into the rubble. He was breathing, from the looks of it barely, and out cold.
Darius’s chest heaved with fury as he felt his temper rising. “That’s it,” he growled.
The plaza cracked beneath his feet as he went in hard, fists searing with pure Volcanic energy, every strike a quake of rage.
The thing tried to absorb his energy, but recoiled in pain.
Overhead a long shadow fell, a scream of light cut through the haze. Helios King had arrived. His descent shattered the air and his weapon hit dead centre. For a second the creature sloshed. It reformed with a screech of anger and pain.
Eira stared, face pale. “What is this thing? Some new generation Dreg?”
King didn’t answer; instead, he turned, scanning the field, his expression unreadable.
“We need coordination. Now. And I need Kaelith up and off the field.”
Seren stirred, coughing once. “I’m good. I—”
He wasn't and Eira didn’t wait. “Med-shuttle. Now.” she snapped.
Within seconds, a field medic pod zipped in, locking Seren in and vanishing up into the sky. No parting joke, no cocky salute, just his worried eyes lingering as he disappeared from sight.
The monster writhed in response, its mass growing more volatile as if it felt the shift in attention. The team surged forward led by King, Eira beside him, with Darius crashing in behind. This time the hits landed; blades of light, molten fists, as frost shards dug deep into the sludge. The thing wailed but it didn’t fall. Instead it lashed out; a jagged spike of matter shot out at King faster than lightning. It struck flesh in King’s shoulder and the golden light leaked in sharp rivulets, sizzling as it touched the ground. For the first time, King looked surprised as he clutched his shoulder in thinly veiled pain.
Darius didn’t hesitate. He roared and drew all his energy, all his fury. The move was forbidden for use in populated sectors. It destabilised structures, boiled air, shattered bones. But this part of the shopping annex was already gone, and they were losing.
The air turned to fire and Darius hit the monster with a full Volcanic surge. The creature shrieked, the plaza caved inward towards them slightly from the vacuum power of his blow.
And then there was nothing. The sludge imploded into itself leaving no corpse, no remains. Just a tremor in the ground and a sickly silence.
Eira rushed to King, hands already glowing cold. She froze and sealed the wound expertly. King didn’t even wince in protest, his gaze still fixated on where the monster had imploded.
Citizens who hadn’t been able to flee were beginning to edge forward, shell-shocked and looking for answers. Eira raised her hands and sent a gentle wave of cleansing frost over the area, cooling the molten floor and hiding some of the mess. It shimmered with beauty and helped ease the unbearable heat.
Darius stood still, eyes hard. “Something is very wrong,” he said between his teeth. “That was NOT a Dreg.”
King didn’t look at him, just stared into the distance, jaw clenched.
“Maybe it was a Voided?” Eira offered. “An escapee?”
“None reported,” Darius seethed, trying to control his temper and let his body return to normal before moving.
King turned, voice like stone. “Check the Nexus logs. Cross-reference undocumented mutations that match anything like this. I want a report on my desk before your next patrol.”
The silence remained heavy as the clean-up began. Med-pods descending in droves, sterile white tech teams emerging from shielded carriers with blinking scanners and probing eyes. The once bustling ForgeDeep shopping nexus now lay fractured in emergency stasis with energy nets starting to converge.
Eira stayed near King until the field seal finished cooling on his shoulder. He said nothing but when his wound was completely frozen shut he turned and without a word disappeared up into the haze.
Eira knelt, unfazed and started recording data from the fractured plaza. Her voice was calm but clipped, relaying everything she could remember into her com-tech. Mutation level, regeneration speed, power. Her hands trembled slightly when she thought no one was looking.
Darius waited patiently where he was, back straight, jaw locked, letting the ground beneath solidify and cool before trying to move over it. Once he was sure he wouldn’t melt the ground by walking he walked the length of the broken square, his eyes scanning, reading every fracture, every mark cut into the stone. A tremor pulsed faintly under his boots from the damaged infrastructure and he felt a small pang of guilt as he looked toward the service access shafts.
They should be sealed but he knew what lay below. The Undercity. But certainly not from there to here? That thing had not gotten this far without tripping a single alert?
Impossible.
He turned back slowly. Something wasn’t right; none of the pieces were fitting and it was starting to piss him off.


***


By the time he got back to the AAN, his body ached from exertion but his mind was wired and sharp. He didn’t speak or head to the showers. Just walked straight to the public relay wall and pulled up the latest broadcast feed.
The headline spun slowly above a polished newscaster’s smile:
“MINOR CONTAINMENT DISRUPTION IN THE FORGE SECTOR. NO CIVILIAN CASUALTIES. AAN ASSURES FULL CONTROL.”
Darius stared at the screen, unblinking. He’d nearly torn the plaza apart trying to stop that thing. Eira’s flames had been all but useless and the General had actually taken a hit. He couldn’t even remember the last time that happened. And they were calling it minor?
He stood in the middle of the room, the buzz of terminals washing over him like static. They were hiding it. And lying.
Beside the usual facade of control, he wasn’t sure why. Dregs went wild all the time. But he felt it, deep in his bones. Something big was coming. He turned and headed for the sparring halls. If they weren’t going to tell them what threat he had to face, then he had to be ready for anything. Something had shifted on Titan; he could feel it.
________________


















Chapter 4
The Undercity wind howled down the exposed alleyways as Myth and Lyra stepped back through the outer corridor of the Big O, still laughing. Their most recent run had gone surprisingly smooth: no panic, no double-backs. Just some stolen tech, a slick detour through a collapsed rail tunnel, and a narrow escape from a glitched-out security drone that tried to marry Lyra on sight.
Lyra elbowed her playfully. “You know,” she said, grinning, “for someone who didn’t even remember her own name a half-cycle year ago, you’ve got some decent instincts.”
Myth chuckled. “I’ve got decent survival trauma. Same difference.”
They shoved the heavy door open, still laughing. A few Dregs looked up at them, but what really caught Myth off guard was Vexal. He was standing near the workbench as usual, but he was smiling. Sort of.
“I’ve got a surprise for you degenerates,” he said, his voice lazy but unmistakably energised. “It’s a training day.”
Korric, who was sitting against the far wall tinkering with a pulse clamp, looked up sharply. “No shit?”
Vexal didn’t answer. He just tossed him the keys to one of their older transport rigs.
“Load up.”


***


The vehicle was a beast: half-tracked, rust-armoured. Growling, it moved fast and heavy across the jagged ridges beyond the Undercity, away from the last zones of rotted infrastructure and into the deep blight plains.
The sun wasn’t visible here, just the sullen purple green glow of atmospheric burn-off and methane haze curling like fireless smoke across the landscape. But the sky was wide, and for once in what felt like a long while, there were no alarms ringing.
Myth watched from the edge of her seat, legs bouncing slightly, nervous energy twisting in her gut. They arrived at a barren patch of cracked earth and ancient transport bones, like the skeleton of some great space beast left to rot.
“This place is off-grid,” Vexal said, exiting the rig. “No com-techs, no signals. You can wreck the world out here and no one blinks.”
Myth followed the others into the clearing. Her boots sank slightly into the pliant, ash-covered ground. She glanced at Lyra, who had already tossed her jacket aside.
“Watch this,” she said, winking at Myth and extending her hand.
A blast of distorted air shimmered out from her palm, bending the light then shattering it into splintered mirror flashes. She vanished, only to reappear behind Myth with a flourish and a laugh. She then flashed back and forth out of the transport walls with ease. Myth was astonished, but then realised why so many of their missions went as easy as they did. Lyra could walk through walls.
Korric stepped up. “Alright, alright!” He grinned, cracking his knuckles. “Can’t do too much here. Don’t want to cause a rift.” He stomped once and the ground responded violently, groaning as a shockwave split the rock like paper. His body shimmered faintly, the low pulse of Tectonic energy rolling thunderous beneath his skin. Myth had to pull her jaw closed as the delicious surge of energy radiated through her. He had barely moved, yet the devastation was immense.
Then came Vexal. His Rot leaked into the ground with a hiss, curling black tendrils underneath his feet; the air around him hummed like a barely contained storm.
“Your turn,” he said, turning to Myth. “If you’re going to run with us, you need to show us what you can do.”
She hesitated. “I don’t... really know what I can do entirely."
Maybe another monster? No, not here! Her mind was scrambling. She glanced around.
“You’ll figure it out,” Vexal said, then roared a wild, guttural sound and launched himself toward her like a rocket. Myth’s body reacted before her brain could; one blink and she was gone. She reappeared across the field, untouched, the wind from his attack kicking up ash where she’d been just a second before.
Vexal skidded to a halt, disoriented. His head whipped around, looking for her. Myth smiled and waved. The second time he lunged, she was already warping again, this time farther out, blinking between spots like a beam of half-real light.
Lyra squealed from across the field. “She can warp!? What the actual vreck?”
“That is threx! By Aetherion!” Korric whooped.
Myth’s heart raced, not from fear but from exhilaration. Her body felt loose and fast, like her bones had remembered something she hadn’t.
She dropped low, placed her hands against the cracked ground, and felt it shift strongly. The landscape morphed under her touch, two distinct formations rising like warring gods from the dirt. One spiralled and smooth black stone, the other twisted, jagged, and glowing with crimson veins. They stood poised for battle, ready to do her bidding. Even Vexal moved cautiously. She walked between them and let them collapse, blending into a pristine field of blue-green grass with speckled white blowers.
Vexal stumbled, genuinely caught off guard. He growled and slammed his fist into the ground, sending waves of Rot decay shooting towards her. Myth stood tall, opened her hand wide, and absorbed it.
She drank it in, letting it pour into her like molten metal down her spine like a salve. It hurt, it burned, but it lit her up in ways nothing else ever had. She felt invincible, powerful.
Vexal leapt, spinning mid-air with a movement so sharp and vicious she could see Korric take a step forward and hear Lyra swear. But Myth had already moved out of range. Warp and shift. Vexal and his Rot couldn’t keep up. Then, with a sharp twist of her hand, she swapped Korric and Vexal’s places. Mid-air, Korric was gently deposited on the far edge of the field, confused but safe. Vexal was dropped straight into the ground under a storm of focused energy. She rained it down on him, each wave cleaner and sharper than the last. Her eyes shone silver in the reflection of her power; her body was glowing. Vexal laughed up at her, his Rot braced like a shield, his hair whipping around him in the chaos. He was out of breath, ecstatic, and laughing like a maniac.
He launched again and they collided, both at full tilt. He beat her by an inch, pinning her to the field. Their foreheads touched.
“Enough,” he whispered, laughing still.
She was laughing too, her breath short. Around and beneath them pink and white flowers bloomed and the field sang with light. Children had wandered from nearby shelters, watching in awe as the warped battlefield turned into a blooming field of beauty. Lyra was already playing with them, her eyes tear-bright from laughing too hard. Korric tossed a makeshift ball into the air, beaming as he balanced a toddler on his boot.
Vexal pulled Myth to her feet, his hands lingering for a second before he cleared his throat and stepped back.
“I can’t remember the last time I went all out like that,” he muttered, almost looking embarrassed.
“With such a good outcome!” Korric called, grinning ear to ear. They spent the rest of the afternoon with the outskirt families enjoying laughter and games, things normally foreign to the Undercity.
Later, back at the Big O, the celebration carried them late into the night. Myth sat beside a young cook who showed her an old photo on a cracked data-pad: fried noodles, pink dumplings, something looking sweet and strange. After a few questions, Myth smiled and focused. Her energy reformed the mush delicately, using the energy she had absorbed from Vexal she gave life to the mush, turning it into delicious ingredients and recipes. Soon the table was set and that night, for the first time in their lives, everyone in the Big O went to bed with full bellies.
As Lyra hugged her tight in the early-morning hours as she whispered, “I wasn’t sure about you at first... but Myth, you’re one of the best things that’s ever happened to us.”
Korric nodded, a soft smile on his lips. “Agreed.”
They left Myth and Vexal alone in the quiet.
Vexal didn’t say a word at first. He just stared at her, something unreadable behind his eyes. Then, with a tilt of his chin, he turned and jerked his head toward the corridor. Myth followed him silently, heart still thudding in the afterglow of power and shared laughter. He walked her back to her small room, but she had an idea.
She stepped inside, letting him in, then locking the door. Letting the quiet settle, she took a deep breath and focused. The walls shifted under her fingers, the steel smoothing into warmth, a proper bed emerging from the crates like it had always been there. A hanging light blinked to life with a soft amber hue. Next came a real blanket, then a small shelf with a vase of something that looked like small black flowers.
Vexal leaned against the doorway, arms crossed. He nodded, slowly and approvingly. Then he kicked off his boots with a grunt and lowered himself onto the bed. He looked exhausted, but his eyes were still sharp. “How do you feel?” he asked.
She sank down beside him, the bed creaking slightly, hands folded in her lap. “Still really good,” she said truthfully. “Especially after earlier.”
He nodded. “I’ve only used my energy like that a few times in my life. No one’s ever taken a full hit of my Rot before, though. Let alone turned it into something beautiful.”
Myth blinked, slightly off guard. “I didn’t really think about it,” she admitted. “I just... did it.”
Vexal studied her. “So with all your energy,” he said, voice lower now, “what do you want? Do you still not remember anything?”
She shook her head slowly. “No memories. No nothing. And I really want to remember. More than anything. To know where I’m from or who I am.” She let out a half-laugh.
He leaned back on one arm. “And back when you let loose... your monster. How did that feel?”
She hesitated. “I don’t know,” she finally huffed, her hair flipping out of her eyes. “At the time, I was starving. And it just happened. I didn’t mean to let it out. It was like my hands moved before I even thought.”
Vexal reached into the folds of his coat and pulled out a cracked display tablet. He tapped it, and the static filled with old news footage. The attack had been covered briefly by a smaller news station. The images showed the aftermath, the wreckage. But the tone was subdued; the creature she’d made looked like a smudge in the background. It didn’t look like an actual threat to anyone.
“They’re saying it wasn’t that bad,” he said, watching her carefully.
Relief flooded through Myth. “It felt worse. I thought I’d... done something unforgivable.”
“You didn’t,” Vexal said in a matter-of-fact tone. “You saw how unfair it is. That place, those people living clean while the rest of us rot down here? Dregs don’t deserve to be less just because we were born different. But things can change.”
She looked up, eyes bright. “You really believe we can change that?”
“I have to believe it,” he said. “I want to tear it all down and build something new. Something better.”
He tapped again. A new image lit up the screen: cleaner, polished, godlike. General Helios King.
The moment Myth saw him, a sharp, ice-hot spike lanced through her head. She gasped, clutching the edge of the bed. Light. Blinding white light. A soundless scream. Her body fracturing, burning away into nothing. The sky itself opened up, raining barrage after barrage upon her.
“He hated me,” she whispered as the vision faded, holding herself tightly. “I don’t remember anything else... but I could feel it. As he killed me, he hated me for what I was.”
Vexal was already beside her. He didn’t ask permission; he pulled her into his lap and held her close as sobs wracked her body. His arms felt solid and safe, a far cry from the haunted feeling in her soul.
“Do you want revenge?” he asked softly.
Myth’s breath hitched. “Yes,” she said. Then her voice faltered. “But I'm not strong enough. Not against someone like him.”
Vexal didn’t argue. King’s power and energy were undeniable. He was quiet for a long time, but she could feel him thinking. Finally he said, “When you took that hit from me. What did you feel?”
She thought for a second. “I didn’t expect it to feel that good. It was like... the world opened up.”
He was still holding her; she felt his fingers trace up her spine. Then slowly he shifted her so she straddled his lap. Her breath caught in her throat, confused. He took both her hands, his eyes closed.
The cybernetic network on the left side of his face pulsed softly, veins of dark light glowing under the skin. She felt it before he even pushed into her: his energy, dark and wild, threading into her like a second heartbeat.
Her whole body arched into his, her cells responding to his Rot energy like they’d been starving for him.
She moaned involuntarily, her mouth parting slightly. “I... I want more.” She started to draw and couldn’t stop herself. Her fingers curled against his arms, afraid. “What if I... what if you disappear?” she whispered.
He opened his eyes, silver-grey and burning. He took her palms on either side of his face and the power ignited between them, looping his Rot with her Dark energy, twisting and sparking and melting into the air. Tendrils of black and violet energy climbed the walls and curled around them like vines of ruin. The room dimmed and bloomed at the same time.
His lips crashed into hers and stars detonated behind her eyes. Her whole body sang and tingled. She could feel herself becoming stronger, brighter, but more than that, she felt him. Strong, powerful, rising in her.
They broke apart, gasping. The walls shook with the aftermath and a few broken screws fell from a ceiling panel. Vexal was staring at his hand, where a glowing, solid strand of Rot writhed like a living whip.
“What... just happened?” he whispered.
Myth couldn’t answer. She just looked up at him, glowing softly in the low light. He pulled her close again gently and pressed a soft kiss to the top of her head.
“How do you feel?” he asked.
She curled against his chest, closing her eyes and exhaling. “Safe,” she whispered.
He smiled faintly and gently stretched out, her head rising and falling with each breath he took. His arms stayed wrapped around her. She fell asleep just like that, warm and quiet.






















Chapter 5
A sharp knock cracked through the early morning stillness.
“Vexal. We need you out front.” Lyra’s voice came through the door, clipped and tense. Both Myth and Vexal startled awake. He was up in a blink, laying her aside with surprising gentleness. She barely registered the motion before he was shoving his boots on and disappearing through the door.
Myth scrambled upright, her heart racing in her chest. She tossed on her clothes and hurried after him, sliding her feet quickly into her boots, not bothering to lace them up. Something was wrong. She could feel it before she saw it. The main area of the Big O was buzzing like a live wire. Lyra’s jaw was clenched and Korric stood near the entrance, his shoulders trembling. His fists were balled so tight his knuckles had gone ashy.
“What happened?” Myth asked breathlessly.
Lyra didn’t look at her, only pointed at a nearby screen. A flickering holo displayed a smoking blue and green ruin. The flower field burned almost completely to ash. A child's toy, half-melted and still glowing faintly, lay abandoned in the corner of the footage.
“They were kids,” Lyra said, her voice flat. “They were just playing.”
Korric made a strangled sound and suddenly the whole building groaned beneath them, plates shifting. Dust crashed in chunks from the ceiling and lights flickered. Somewhere in the back, a pipe snapped and hissed.
“Myth,” Lyra hissed, grabbing her and Vexal both. “If you can get us out of here in any way, do it. Or we and all of the Undercity are gonna be buried alive.”
Myth didn’t hesitate. She closed her eyes tight and warped, but the image of the flower field was scorched into her mind. The Big O's heat and chaos faded. Cold air and still silence hit her lungs with the sour burn of acrid smoke. When she opened her eyes, they were back. Standing in the heart of the now decimated field.
It was worse in person. The ground was scorched so bad nothing would probably ever grow there again. Blackened air still rose in curling tendrils, the ground sputtered and boiled in places, refusing to cool. Myth looked around, struggling to register what she was seeing.
Korric dropped to his knees, making the gravity plate beneath the district shift again, nearly throwing them off their feet.
“Korrick.” Vexal tried to steady him, gripping his shoulder tight. “We’ll figure this out. But if you lose control now, more will die. We could all die.”
But it was too late; the quake hit them hard and the ground cracked with a thunderous clap. Myth stumbled, heart in her throat and actions rash.
She moved to Korric's side, arms outstretched. Her powers surged with violent grace as she twisted her dark transformation across the shaking plate. The ground responded, re-knitting, sealing, and settling over itself like a shadow poured into broken skin. At the same time, she pressed her hand against Korric’s chest and tried to pull in his energy. It nearly tore her apart. This was not like Vexal’s rot; this was tectonic pressure, core-rippling and brutal. It pounded through her bones, causing her muscles to spasm with every surge. She tasted metal in her mouth and felt her knees buckle. She felt Vexal beside her, holding her, holding them both in his arms. She was in Vexal's arms as Korric shuddered under her hand. The massive man was crying, brokenly whispering names like prayers as tears streaked his craggy face.
Each name gutted her. He knew so many that were gone. It took everything to hold him, to keep their world from being torn apart by his raw pain. Finally, she felt the trembling ground still and silence fell around them like a shroud  as they all sank to the ground together.
The field somehow looked somewhat peaceful again. The turnover of earth helped erase the scarring and it looked almost like it did before the flowers. Korric wiped his face roughly and gave Myth an awkward pat on the head. She smiled faintly, still trying to catch her breath.
“Why?” she whispered.
Lyra was the first to answer, her voice sharp in the wind. “Retaliation,” she spat. “Something beautiful in the Undercity? We can’t be having that. Doesn’t matter if it was completely safe or not.”
“They always take,” Korric murmured. “Anything good, anything that gives us hope, they take.”
Myth felt her jaw tighten, but she couldn’t think, she couldn’t process. They were in an open field but she still felt like air was closing in on her.
Vexal, who had been watching her reaction closely, finally stood facing them with a strange fire in his eyes.
“I’ve been thinking for a long time,” he said slowly. “Planning. I want to take the AAN down. All of it. By any means necessary.”
Silence stretched for a beat. “I’m in.” Lyra’s voice was hard as steel.
Korric didn’t hesitate. “I’ve had enough. We all have.”
Lyra turned to Myth. “Only if you’re in. I’m not going to force anyone into this mess and this isn’t your fight.”
Myth met her gaze then looked at Korric and lastly Vexal. She rose, her head held high. “I want to help. We can do this if we all come together.”
Vexal looked stunned for a beat then he smiled. A real smile, which was rare. He pulled them all in close. A rough, warm hug. There wasn’t a plan yet but they were together.
When Myth warped them all back to the Big O, the place had calmed, and thankfully was still intact. Lyra flopped onto a nearby chair with a groan. “I swear, if we had you before, we’d have avoided so much running.”
Korric gave a soft chuckle in agreement before his face twisted into a frown. “Power like yours? No way that flies under any radar for long.”
Vexal turned to Myth, his face thoughtful. “Can you make your room… bigger?”
Myth thought hard; she really didn’t know. Leading them all back to her room, she ran her fingers along the wall, concentrating. The metal bucked silently, folding open and reshaping. The room expanded steadily under her fingertips. She focused on what they would need: couch, rug, table, light. A string of warm lights hummed gently overhead and Lyra squealed, immediately jumping on the plush couch.
“Now this!” she hummed, settling in deep. “Is the most comfy I think I’ve been in years.”
Korric mumbled in agreement, settling in near her, leaning his back against the couch and closing his eyes.
Myth plopped down on the rug but Vexal remained standing in the centre, gaze focused in a way she hadn’t seen before. “Alright,” he said, rubbing his hands together. “If we are going to do this, we have to do it right. Even with Myth’s powers. Actually, almost worse, because she raises so many red flags and they would rather have a system-wide, complete lock-down than lose power to a band of revolutionaries."
Lyra huffed. “Yeah, they would probably retaliate again against Dregs as a whole if they found even a whiff that someone like her could be from here.”
“She could become one,” Korric said quietly. “An Apex. Infiltrate them?”
Vexal nodded slowly. “Right now the AAN’s front men, Voxx and King, are true power players. Ignoring that would be foolhardy and we only get one chance at this. Nobody has tried to infiltrate the AAN directly before so I doubt they’ll have their guards up. But if she was able to get in close, either by causing division or learning of any definitive weaknesses…”
“We could take them down on both fronts,” Myth finished.
“Between the four of us they already would have a hard time,” Lyra said. “But this would be not only the safest but in the long run probably the quickest.”
“We cannot withstand a drawn-out war,” Vexal affirmed. “That’s already been tried before and it ended badly. The Dregs can’t take another loss like that one or else they might just wipe us off the map.”
Myth couldn’t help but wonder what happened but she bit her lip. Another time. “But how would I become one of them? And who is ‘them’ exactly?” she asked the most pressing question on her mind.
Vexal grinned a cunning grin, pulling a device from his pocket. “Alright Myth, we are going to teach you everything you need to know to become an Apex.”






















Chapter 6
The following weeks blurred by like light through cracked glass, beautifully broken and much too fast. The Big O pulsed with new life as the seasons changed, and each new cycle week brought new recruits and new hope. What began as four outcasts plotting in the Undercity expanded into something bigger and stronger and, by the end of cycle week three, a real rebellion.
They came in ones and twos, then threes and fours and Myth tried to help everyone she could. Tarja, a girl with purple filaments of living blades snaking across her skin. Twitching with every heartbeat, every move. She didn’t speak much and her eyes carried the constant weight of the pain she was in. Myth had sat up with her one night, palm to shoulder, trying to feel her energy even though she didn’t fully understand herself. She felt her own mutation settling across the chaos inside the girl, smoothing her nerves like a cold salve. The blades began to retract one by one, and soon Tarja was left with smooth skin; only slight scarring remained as proof of her latent rampaging mutation. Tarja had smiled and hugged her, crying and laughing. It was the first time she had felt human touch without hurting the other person. Myth was almost as overwhelmed as she was joyful. There were hundreds of stories just like Tarja’s; from Dregs to Luminal-tier, she helped all who asked her.
Vexal had watched the whole thing, his face unreadable. When Myth stood up, breathless and swaying slightly, he caught her gently by the elbow. “You can help them,” he murmured. “Just be careful.” His voice was rough with something she couldn’t quite name.
After that, he had let her only help the worst cases. The ones who were one step away from being voided. Those who could not go more than five steps without screaming in pain. She loved helping, and each one settled into a special place in her heart. But it cost her dearly. Her hands would shake for hours afterward, her eyes bleeding faint silver tears. Vexal did his best to stop her, but even he couldn’t ignore the cries of the broken.
Evenings turned into training drills. Endless hours of practice runs to forge her into the Apex Elite they needed her to be. They worked her mind first. The history of Titan. Speech cadence. AAN doctrine. Names and ranks. Her own new identity: Nyx. Her forged records were slotted seamlessly into the system, stitched together by whoever Vexal’s contact on the inside was. She was now an undocumented elite from a sealed Verdant lab. The elites kept their children off the records and hidden for privacy apparently as normal occurrences, so her showing up now was actually on target. Her powers, though, made even that unlikely. So they tailored them. Origin: “classified.” Mutation: Dark Transformation. Truthful enough but vague enough not to raise any flags too early. Warping was entirely off the record. Absorption rate they left as redacted. Whatever she did at the Dreg field? Never happened. No one needed to know how far she could go.
Fighting and combat training turned out to be mostly a waste of time anyways. Even with her powers crippled and her most powerful moves completely off the table, Dark Transformation was impossible to reckon with standing alone while remaining under the radar. Vexal tried half-heartedly, as did Lyra, knowing that their mutation types were just too much of a mismatch. Korric, however, went all out and still ended up on his back with the wind knocked out of him. So instead they taught her restraint—to keep control, to not let her temper get the best of her. Because the Apex Corps would test her in ways that strength and power alone couldn’t win.
And then suddenly it was the night before the Apex Entrance Trials. Everything else had been submitted, the exams aced; she was ready. The final piece was tomorrow, and it was the most dangerous one.
Lyra had already gone to bed, mumbling something about how her nerves needed extra dreamspace before tomorrow. Korric lingered just long enough to lay a hand on her shoulder before disappearing into the hallway.
It was just the two of them finally in the silence. Myth sat on the edge of the couch, arms wrapped around her knees, the light from the lamp behind her catching in her hair turning the black a deep velvet blue hue. Vexal stood by the table, still going over her gear, making sure that all the codes were authenticated in the system one last time. He didn’t say anything while Myth watched him finish up and come sit on the couch beside her, leaning back and kicking his boots off. For a long second he sat, then asked simply, “You ready?”
Myth nodded out of reflex, but it was hardly convincing. Vexal turned to her, an expression she couldn’t decipher disturbing the shadows of his scarred face. “Answer me,” he said softly. “But this time, don’t lie.”
She blinked hard, feeling her throat tighten at his intense eye contact. “No,” she whispered.
