She rolled over, turning into Darius’s chest, her tears falling gently down her face. She pulled him in tight as his arms tightened around her, the ache in her heart finally soothing.
The weeks blurred into months, the cycle-year finally bleeding into the cold and colourless winter. Titan had changed rapidly, the days growing ever shorter while the snow and ice tore through the sectors.
She walked with Darius, bundled into her winter gear, her mind hardly focusing on the uneventful patrol. The new wave of propaganda ads tore through the atmospheric dome silently above them, Helion Prime’s daily hero ad playing before the morning sun rose. Each plaza they patrolled and every route pulsed with bright banners displaying hero-forward slogans. Her mind drifted back to the frenzy Titan went into once Voxx had announced the Hero Guard. Within weeks, state-sponsored Hero Agencies sprang up around Titan like spores. Their crystal doors were guarded by T.E.B. bots, their recruiters handing out neon pamphlets on every street corner in every sector.
She had spent the last two weeks at the Halflight Hero Agency in the Outer Pulse of Blackroot Verge. She trained wayward Echelons and up-and-coming Dregs, passing on the mandatory energy training lessons that the AAN mandated every hero know. Lyra was installed as the Director, the number of sympathisers quietly legitimising Dreg-led patrols. Her close proximity made it easier for Myth to get messages from Vexal. She hadn’t dared go back to see him in person, tracker out and all, she didn’t trust Dr. Ekros or Fane to keep their word.
Even with the flood of heroes, the attacks didn't stop, and Dreg spin-outs were on the rise. Thankfully, there were no more of Fane’s monsters; the streets were swarmed with smaller ones. Seemingly failed experiments left to run wild, tearing through civilian living sectors mindlessly. Criminals wasted no time in capitalising on the chaos, robbing and attacking wilfully while the heroes were left to try to mitigate the damage.
Myth was exhausted mentally and physically. As the monster attacks increased, so did the AAN’s might. All official Apex units were outfitted with mutation-supportive gear and tremendous weapons. Darius wielded twin Magma-tantalum carbide hammers, their faces decoratively forged with Darius’s own energy; most average monsters didn’t even stand a chance against one of his blows.
She had been sanctioned twin sickles made from an amalgam of her own ribs and energy. Their dark, serrated edges moved through the air like liquid, a constant, swirling barrier around her body, constantly regulating her energy, increasing her output use after use. Kill after kill.
Each completed patrol, she felt herself becoming more numb and confused. Fane’s words constantly swirled with the memories of Voxx calling her name when he was dying. He never gave her a second glance or word; she had instinctively lain low the first few weeks, thinking that her cover was surely blown. But nothing. It was confusing and, for some reason, painful. The memories or hallucinations she thought she had when she was dying had faded quickly into nothingness, leaving her never-ending questions.
That evening, she sat at Darius’s side, another opulent dinner underway. Another night of mandatory team-building disguised as a dinner. Endless platters of priceless food and a sea of drink, it always shocked her after leaving the poorer and middle sectors, where the average civilian barely had enough food for the day. And it wasn’t choice food either. Eating anything these days was purely a performative chore to keep the truth of her mutation quiet. Only a handful of people still knew, and she wanted to keep it that way indefinitely.
As the night wound down, Darius left her to volunteer for the night patrol in Forgedeep. It was an area that had been hit relentlessly by Vexal and the Rebellion in the never-ending struggle for resources and weapons. She found herself retiring to her own quarters night after night, the cold silence somehow comforting, even though her eyes stayed wide open.
“Go to the Helios Vault if you think you are ready to know. See it for yourself.”
Fane’s words echoed in her ears, her mind running through all possibilities and traps he could have set, even if it did seem unlikely. Something about the conversation, or maybe his eyes, told her he wasn’t lying. But why would he tell her the truth?
She had already located the Vault during a standard archival processing sweep for the Helix Spire after all the damage. Nobody wanted to go through the twelve-hour-long process, and she had been alone for a majority of it. The Helios Vault was a highly restricted medical archive hidden deep in the Frostline Divide. Unreachable on foot and riddled with Glacier Maws. Maybe Fane was sending her there to be killed, she mused. Between the cold and the lack of oxygen that far out, she had to prepare for her flight. She would have simply warped in, but every high-access vault had energy suppressors and alarm systems if they read too high an energy index, and she certainly didn’t want the entire AAN raining down on her.
She tossed and turned, mulling it over in her mind, tonight would be the perfect night… It had been an easy day, and Voxx hadn’t needed her, choosing to synergise with Lynx, to Myth’s relief. She glanced at her comtech; it was still relatively early in the night, so she could make it there and back with hours to spare to search for information. Her cold-cycle gear came with a breathing mask; all she needed was some warm, non-AAN clothing.
She rolled over again. This is crazy, she thought with a frustrated sigh. She had no idea of the layout once inside, and she would be close to powerless if something happened. She didn’t even dare bring her comtech, instead having memorised the lowest-pressure route. She decided she would fly in, using her sickles until she was close enough to see the entrance, then continue on foot.
She stood resolutely and walked into her closet. There was no reason she couldn’t warp at least to the edge of the Cryothorne district, that would shorten her trek from Helion massively.
Myth suited up carefully in the dark, her fingers shaking slightly as she clipped the pieces of her dark-weather gear into place. She kept her breathing slow, taking full advantage of the clean air while she could. The southeastern glacial stretch of Titan was mostly sub-zero temperatures and rough, inhospitable terrain. She fitted her breathing mask to her face carefully, the humidifier immediately steaming up her face. With one last check-over, she closed her eyes and focused, the cold of the nightmare horizon bursting around her as she landed hard.
Her armour immediately whitened, the tech reacting to the icy wind blowing down from Cryothorne. She took a deep breath, steadying herself before pulling her sickles from their hip harnesses.
She was standing on the Frostline Shelf, a jagged ice wasteland that fractured into canyons and fissures in a constant ocean of glaciers grinding against one another. The sound tore through the air, her bones rattling slightly from the rumbling ground. That was the least of her problems.
Crystal-wolves. Massive beasts with razor-sharp fins and six eyes, they hunted in packs of hundreds and went berserk at the scent of warm blood. Myth felt a shiver of fear as she saw their hides catch in the faint light, and the air thrummed with their mating calls. Further out, Glacier Maws broke through the cascading waterfall of avalanches, creating their own streams in brutal displays of dominance against the wolves. They could reach up to twenty metres long and weigh up to sixty tonnes of blind hatred. She gritted her teeth; she couldn’t warp farther not knowing where she was going, but she could make small warped jumps. She took a deep breath and started stealthily making her way across the frozen ocean, keeping her energy low and only warping within sight.
She wasn’t even a kilometre in before the first wolf stalked her, the light refracting in soft blues and pinks off its crystal hide. She spun hard, her sickles flashing over the ice, slicing brutally through its throat. She just missed its jugular, and it shrieked in pain, the rest of the pack responding in kind, bearing down on her.
She ran for it, warping as fast as she could across the ice, dodging in and out of packs to confuse the beasts, the scent of her sweat causing them to frenzy. She was out of breath by the time she made it to the far ridge, the Maws carving her way up the mountain, the wolves hot on her tail. She pushed harder, trying to put more distance between herself and danger as the ridge steepened.
She almost passed the Vault, its single black door hidden in a fissure of ice. She skidded to a stop and frantically started clearing the ice and snow with the hilt of her sickle, the howls of the wolves bearing down on her. She felt sweat prick her neck as she slammed her gloved fingers into the panel, trying to punch in the archive code, but the old screen flickered slowly, the ice and age finally catching up with it.


ACCESSING: PLEASE WAIT


She wanted to scream as the howling got closer, the ground shaking violently from their approach. She turned and looked to see they were less than a kilometre away and closing fast, the chaos of two Glacier Maws closing in behind them, no doubt ready to take advantage of the bloodshed.
She bit her lip hard and turned, her breath coming fast and hard, the screen still blinking like a beacon in the dark at her.


ACCESS GRANTED


The Vault groaned open slowly, the internal gears sounding like a scream, spurring the wolves on. Her heart was slamming in her chest as she pushed her body through the slowly opening door, immediately slamming her palm on the close-control button as she slid inside.
The door slowly began to close again, a wolf's face shoving through the narrow gap, its jaws snapping just inches from her. Not daring to draw her energy weapons, she watched as the door slowly crushed its head, the beast howling in pain as the door sealed with a seismic boom, a shower of ice and blood raining down.
She sat there for a moment, her chest heaving as the air mask tried to keep up, the howls outside turning into chaos as the wolves feasted on the other end of the dead wolf. She stood up, the light from the Vault flickering as the generators booted up slowly.




























Chapter 40
Her boots crunched loudly against the frosted tiles, the temperature inside the Vault much cooler than outside. The Helios Vault was even more vast than she’d imagined, its three spires displayed brilliantly in the entrance, outlining her current location. Shaped like a crown from above, its three spires branched out through Titan’s crust. One to Aetherion, one into Nocturne, and one to her, Cryothorne. A faint light fogged on instantly, illuminating steel signage above her, directing her to the elevators. Her gaze swept the lower floor, catching faint, scattered feathers and massive boot prints. Voxx had been here recently. The thought sent another shiver down her spine as she followed his steps to the elevator, hesitating for a moment before selecting Floor One: Extraction Archives. There was no way to know what Voxx had been doing here or why, and she didn’t have time to investigate.
The elevator was swift, opening silently into a massive floor of darkened terminals. Row upon row, hundreds if not thousands of terminals gridded across the entire area. Only the one at the front was lit, dimly pulsing from the geothermal converters keeping the electricity on. She moved carefully, mindful of the frost underfoot, and as she got closer, the terminal bleeped alive, greeting her with a loud ping.
She breathed a sigh of relief, a search terminal, the search query blank and blinking blue. She typed quickly, “Myth,” and hit search. The screen blinked off, leaving her in the dark, before she heard a shuddering in the back. A terminal blinked on brightly, steam rolling off it quietly as it warmed up. Myth almost ran to the back, her heart beating in her throat as her eyes immediately started scanning the screen. The file slowly loaded, a picture populating as the information filled in. Her stomach churned violently. It was a picture of her, her eyes closed and hair tucked back, lying flat out on a surgical table, wires protruding from every available patch of skin. She tore her eyes away from the picture and quickly read through the information. It directed to her origin assignment in another vault: Vault 07, in the Obsidian Annex in Nocturne. She frowned. Another dangerous trek? Was this just a wild chase? Her eyes scanned through the rest, but it was sparse. Each entry linked to additional files, all stored in separate sections. Her eyes roamed over the array of dark terminals around her; she couldn’t help but wonder if every terminal held a life. She backed away from the terminal and it went dim, leaving her to rush in the dark back to the elevator. She selected the next closest location on the directory, Floor 2B: Genetic Archives.
The Genetic Archives were colder; deep in the icy rock, the room smelled faintly of burnt plastic and formaldehyde. The same mass of terminals filled the front of the floor, but the back was a huge, ruptured bio-tank. Spiderwebbed cracks encasing the wall behind it, frozen liquid covering the base and parts of the floor. Her stomach churned as she approached the welcome kiosk, typing in her name again. This time the terminal groaned with effort, sifting through the room in a bright array of light before settling on a middle terminal, a video already playing loudly on it.
She rushed over, seeing that the video it displayed was ancient; the shimmering footage showed a group of scientists, all in outdated lab coats, celebrating. A short, round man with a bright red name tag that displayed DR. VYRE was recording the video, his teal gloves waving excitedly through the air.


“Project Myth has produced its first viable offspring!!! It's year nine, and we have the first emergence of a child exposed to raw Catalyst radiation, the human embryo mutated and then stabilised! We did it!”


Her throat constricted, the lack of oxygen making it doubly hard to breathe. The next video auto-played, a large heading moving across the top. It showed hundreds of rows upon rows of embryo vials, some completely blackened.


1ST GENERATION APEX MUTATION // ALIEN CATALYST ENERGIES IMPRINTED ONTO MODIFIED DNA RESULTING IN MATTER-YIELDING TRANSMUTATION HYBRIDISATION (“MYTH”)


The video continued as time lapsed, showing the eggs developing into full embryos, then foetuses. Some were normal; others were grotesquely mutilated. The terminal displayed scientific information she didn’t understand, ending with the experiment having a mixed outcome. Some subjects lived; the rest were non-viable.
She watched as Dr. Vyre extracted a foetus, the procedure brutal and invasive, and inserted it into a nutrient-rich synthetic uterus. The foetus developed, the time-lapse showing constant, heavy monitoring.
She felt a wave of nausea crash over her as she backed away from the terminal. This was her origin. A product of horrific experiments and a test subject in a never-ending quest for power and control? What happened after? Where did Voxx come in? She bit back a scream of frustration, her body shaking violently from the cold. She couldn’t go to the Obsidian Annex. She needed to find out where Voxx had gone while she was here. She turned on her heel and stalked back to the elevator. Her oxygen was getting low, and she still needed enough to make it back to Helion, but she wasn’t leaving without answers. Her fingers hesitated over the floor controls; she didn’t have time to go to every single floor. She looked at the directory again and selected the last one, Floor B9: Cryo-Stasis & Disposal.
The floor dropped, her heart rushing as they descended into the Vault, the air becoming noticeably thinner. She stopped abruptly, the elevator doors opening with a heavy groan. The temperature plummeted the moment she stepped out, the frostbitten silence broken only by the soft hum of the failing lights. Frost crawled the walls like ivy, and the air smelled heavily of ancient antiseptic. She stepped carefully across the frozen floor, her boots crunching on shattered test tubes and miscellaneous scientific paraphernalia, until she could see the bulk of the room. The walls were lined with blackened, cloudy chambers, lined like rows of tombstones, some still sealed with long-dead figures floating inside. At the centre of the chamber sat a large, desk-style terminal that had already flickered to life, a sputtering voice whispering, “Wel-l-l-l-com-m-me, Doc-oc R……”
Her heart was pounding as she approached it slowly, the index already populating with files as her eyes scanned the cracked screen.
Names. Thousands of names. Some she even recognised: Voxx, T., Ekros, V., and Dryst, F. And then her name, thousands of times. Reiterations of her name, variants of her name, her name enumerated: Mythica, Myth-004, M-133D. It seemed like it went on forever. She clicked on Myth-300; her own face, mutilated and starved, appeared on the screen, clearly dead. Glaring red words flashed underneath: STATUS: TERMINATED. A litany of gruesome details followed, and she quickly backed out, her hand shaking violently. She clicked another, and another. Each one, TERMINATED. Different faces, different stages of living to be mutilated. Some missing eyes, arms, the whole bottom halves of their bodies, only their heads remaining. All different ages, from embryo to infant, adolescent, to adult.
Her pulse was screaming in her ears, her chest heaving as she fought the urge to faint. If she passed out here, she would freeze to death down here. Her fingers were already completely numb, her arms heavy from the lack of oxygen in her blood. Frozen tears pricked her eyes as she typed her name into the search: simply Myth. One file populated: M-001X. The terminal rattled and clicked loudly, the screen retracting to display a documentation hologram. A hunched old man sat with a datapad, his shrivelled face comically framed by large, red-rimmed glasses and his hands covered in gaudy teal gloves. He was wearing a bloody lab coat and he looked exhausted. The title at the bottom of the screen read:


PROJECT MYTH: DR. VYRE DISCOVERY LOG AAN COURT MANDATE: PRIMARY APEX DEVELOPMENT NOTES FOR CYCLE 9 TO 47


The terminal buzzed and hissed in the cold as the playback began in a flicker of static and distortion. The first scene was a young Dr. Vyre, his face eager and eyes bright, speaking rapidly:


“My name is Dr. Aerin Eos Vyre. Subject M-001X’s initial designation was singular: an Apex-class stasis embryo directly exposed to the Catalyst Engine brought aboard the E.S. Daedalus Prime. The only Earth-launched ark that reached Titan thanks to, as we know, the Catalyst Engine.”


The footage cut to a sharp, metallic birthing pod on display in a white, sterile room; inside, a tiny infant with black iridescent skin floated in a thick fluid. Electrodes were wrapped around its arms and torso like vines, and a faint heartbeat echoed through the chamber.


“Early attempts to create safe mutation classes via natural gestation were largely… unsuccessful. The original embryo batch suffered a loss of eighty-seven percent. Out of those, only one survived full gestation: Subject M-001X.”


The screen cut to a different room, and she watched herself as a toddler, skin faded and dull, still wrapped in wires. She was watching something out of frame, silent, her eyes dead.


“Unfortunately, the fusion of human and alien DNA created a metabolic hunger similar to the Catalyst Well. No synthetic nutrients can satisfy the energy deficit. Solution: to feed the anomaly energy directly. Subject put in stasis for continued genetic cloning. Clone variants unstable, original Myth Project DNA non-replicable.”


The room shown was full of living versions, all variations of her, starving and huddled at the far end of the room. At the front, she was being held in a heavy metal muzzle, her hands tugging at dampener restraints so hard her wrists bled. A teal hand held her by her hair, typing in an unlock sequence before leaving the room. The muzzle dropped, and screams erupted as she tore through the room, blood and energy clouding the screen viscerally.


“Trial 2: The bottom twenty-three percent of variations starved to see energy consumption rate. Subject Myth encouraged to consume the offered genetic material. Responses observed in mutation: increased thirty-two percent energy stress output. Recovered for reconditioning.”


It went on to show more experiments, Dr. Vyre’s voice aging with her as he experimented on everything she ever was.


“Trial 14: Left arm removed from Subject Myth-116. Rejection noted, but pain threshold increased twelve percent. Recovered for reconditioning…… Trial 31: Isolation protocol enforced for all subjects. Time duration: one hundred and twenty consecutive full cycle-years. No stimuli of any sort. Result of experiment: unfavourable……”


He droned on through hundreds of trials as their numbers dwindled, all signed off by Dr. Vyre, his name slowly changing to just Dr. E. Her hands trembled violently, her body screaming in pain from the cold, the weight of the doctor's words pressing down on her.


“Subjects reawakened under AAN directive. The Red Meridian Project, Cycle Year 208. Subject Voxx, T. T. is experiencing acute bio-system failure due to catastrophic somatic overclock. Initiating immediate re-sequencing of Project M. Medical Asset M-001X has been deployed from preservation matrix to commence in-situ biological stabilisation.”


A young Voxx flashed briefly before her eyes, a pulse deep in her skull pulsing frantically against her spine as she watched, unable to tear her eyes away from the brutal scene in front of her.


“Project Voxx, T. T. exhibits consistent mutation degradation. Subject M-001X physical repair rate: twenty-nine percent. Synergised repair rate: ninety-two percent. Flags: their neural harmonisation is unpredictable at best, with severe emotional deviation.”


The video was flickering wildly now; the facility was in burning chaos and partially destroyed. Confused variants were wandering around in the rubble. She didn’t see herself in the footage, and the doctor's voice crackled on.


“Cycle Year 212.6: Subject M-001X attempted containment breach, succeeding in her effort of releasing all clone variants and Subject Voxx. Riot conditions resulted, and Project Voxx escaped containment with seven viable clones. Subject M-001X recovered for extreme reconditioning. Remaining subjects terminated.”


The voice was clearly Dr. Ekros’s now, showing an animalistic and abused subject, his voice carrying a cheery lilt to it:


“Cycle Year 214: Project M reinstated due to the return of Subject Voxx. Subject cited extreme physical and mutative degradation, was near cessation. Attempted forcible reintegration of Subject M-001X. Due to complete memory loss, reintegration unsuccessful with catastrophic results. Project Myth and Subject M-001X permanently decommissioned. Escaped variants never recovered, presumed dead.”


The screen finally died, the light above flickering wildly before shutting off, throwing the entire chamber into darkness.
She couldn’t see; she couldn’t think. The cold had finally eaten through her gear, and her bones felt like weighted lead. Panic clawed up her throat as her breaths became shorter and faster, and she felt her legs give out from under her as her vision blurred.
A bright, searing orange cut through the dark, and she heard heavy boots on metal. “Myth!”
She blinked hard, her vision sharpening just enough to make out Darius, his hair frosted from the cold as his body steamed in the dim light. He picked her up off the frozen floor, his heat pouring into her body. He quickly replaced her breathing mask with his own, a rush of fresh air reviving her senses. Relief and guilt flooded through her, and she tried to speak, her words slightly stuttered still. “I— I’m sorry—”
“Not now.” His voice was short as he pulled her quickly to the elevator. “The Crystal-wolves are frenzied at the door from smelling warm blood and energy. Twice over. We need to leave.”
She didn’t say anything as Darius moved through the Vault to the front door, the carnage of the dead wolf charred away from the threshold. He slammed his fingers into the control panel, his agitation rolling off him in waves. The seal broke with a hiss, and the arctic wind blasted at them like a physical wall. He unleashed a blast of pure energy into the waiting flood of wolves, shrieks echoing out into the storm as they fled from the heat and light.
Red lights strobed through the Vault, a loud alarm voicing out coldly:


HIGH ENERGY SECURITY ALERT. LOCKDOWN IN PROGRESS.


“Move!” Darius barked before hauling her out into the bleak night. The crashing avalanches roared as the shelf moved in the wind, and she could barely make out Darius’s transport hovering just above the ice. The engine light was flickering wildly, and Darius didn’t hesitate. He picked her up, heat pouring from his body as he launched forward, the rush of air and ice so cold her face stiffened. Wolves leapt behind him in the shadows, and she swung one arm free, slashing through their crystalline skulls with a blade of raw energy. The ice cracked beneath Darius’s feet as he tumbled into the transport, the doors sealing behind them immediately. The transport walls flashed red as they slid sideways, the wolves howling and scratching at the hull. Darius immediately engaged the autopilot, and they leapt above the icy frenzy, soaring towards Helion Prime.
Myth sat curled in her seat, the seat warmer on the highest setting, her body finally starting to register the pain again. Her muscles ached and her lungs spasmed from the oxygen crash. Her hands shook, her fingers screaming in pain from the frostbite. The breathing mask Darius had given her sat loosely in her lap next to her depleted one, another painful reminder of how close she’d come to dying, again.
She didn’t dare look at Darius, who remained standing by the control panel, his back turned to her, arms crossed tightly with his armour hissing at the vents.
The longer he stood there, the heavier the silence grew, and she shifted in her seat finally. “Darius. I’m sorry,” she started tentatively.
He sighed heavily, not turning to her. “You went to the Helios Vault in the height of not only the winter cycle, but Crystal-wolf mating season.”
She flinched at his words, trying to explain herself, but he continued on.
“You triggered one of the entry failsafes. I had to leave people in freezing weather, leaving helping families to rush back here early. Because you wouldn’t answer a comtech beacon.” He turned finally, uncrossing his arms. “It seems like I am always one step behind your lies.”
“That’s rich,” she shot back, her ire finally rising. “You say that like you didn’t let Ekros put a collar in me!”
Darius blinked. “What?”
“You really didn’t know that they put a tracker in my spine where I couldn’t even use my powers without being electrocuted?!”
He shook his head slowly. “No. I didn’t. Ekros said it was an energy stabiliser and only temporary. I would have never agreed to something like an energy collar.”
“I just thought because…” she started, her voice faltering. “I thought you didn’t trust me.”
“Thought what? No. You don’t get to do this. You have lied to me time and time and time again. You have been sneaking around for months, and honestly, I’m insulted you think I wouldn’t notice. I have lied for you, covered for you, I have rescued you from the brink of death over and over.” His voice cracked. “Every time I save you, Myth, it costs me. And for what? Another lie? Because you think I don’t trust you? What is there to trust? Where are you going? Who are you seeing? Whatever convinced you to go into one of the most dangerous regions to enter a dead Vault? Why, Myth?”
She wanted to tell him; she felt tears spill down her cheeks as she felt the transport walls close in on her. “I… I can’t tell you.”
She felt the fire flare off him, his anger rising.
“You can’t tell me? I have never asked you for anything! We are supposed to trust each other with our lives, but it seems like I’ve been the only one trusting.”
She blinked hard, batting away her tears, finally looking at him. His eyes were dark, the flames in them replaced by a cool black. His face was tight and drawn, a frozen gash near his eye from a Crystal-wolf.
“What are you not telling me, Myth? Please. Is it someone else? Family? Just tell me what is going on.” His words were ragged with pain, and she felt a sob escape her throat.
“I’m so sorry, Darius. I don’t want to lie to you, I just… I just can’t tell you yet. I want to, but I can’t.”
His face twisted, his jaw set into a hard line. He nodded curtly, taking a slow, measured breath before turning back to the control panel, immediately pulling up the terminal and filling out reports.
She wanted to go to him, but she stayed where she was, her body trembling slightly, her tears spilling down her cheeks. She tried to think of something, anything she could tell him. She felt the death knell over what little relationship they had left, and her heart screamed at her, begging her to just tell him. Instead, she tucked her head down, feeling the wall go up between them, what she had learned in the Vault raging in her mind.




















Chapter 41
Vexal quietly closed the back door of the Halflight Hero Agency, the smell of recycled air and antiseptic making his nose sting slightly. Slipping in and out of the city was a big risk these days; the AAN had eyes on every corner now, and his face lined wanted boards in every major sector.
He hadn’t seen Myth since the night the Hero Act had been announced, trusting Alaric and Lyra to keep in touch with her. But Lyra’s last message had been terse: “She seems very off. Admin.”
They couldn’t dare elaborate over a line now, so he had come himself. He made his way quietly through an empty training room, the strong smell of the jungle and steel wafting through the open ceiling. The administration office was a bleak, cramped cube, lit by a single yellowed window. Originally retrofitted to be a power-stabilisation chamber for mutation overflow cases, it was now used for overflow paperwork and a litany of administrative duties. He quietly shut the door behind him without making a sound on the copper dampening composites, their burnished hue laced with errant vines.
Myth sat facing the window on a makeshift bench, hunched over a low desk cluttered with datapads and scattered mission logs. Her hair was in a frazzled bun on top of her head and her uniform was dishevelled. He paused, not sure how to get her attention without startling her, when she looked up abruptly, her body jolting slightly with shock.
He took a step forward into the room, the green-tinged fluorescent buzz mixing with the golden glow of the sun filtering through the window. Her silver eyes were red-rimmed and dull, and her nose and hands were unusually red and chapped. Her face and knuckles showed clear signs of the earliest stages of frostbite. He sighed deeply, dread washing over him. “You went to the Vault.”
She nodded, her tears spilling out in twinkling rivulets. He looked away, his jaw locked tight. He had already been to the Vault, seen the experiments, learned about Vyre, Fane, Ekros… everything. When Myth had first shown up on his doorstep, he had started digging, and after Fane and Ekros became so obsessed with owning her, he had made it his mission to find out anything he could about her origin. And all he found was carnage. So he had stopped. Dropped it completely and hoped that she would focus on killing King.
“I think we need to talk about it,” he said finally, sitting down beside her gently. She didn’t answer; instead, she folded her arms tightly around herself, shaking her head slightly.
“Myth. I—”
“Please. Don’t,” she whispered, her tears coming heavier now.
He slid closer, her shoulder touching his chest.
“I’m so tired, Vexal. I just want to stop feeling—” Her breath hitched as she whirled on him, her hands clawing at his clothes.
“No, stop! Listen to me, Myth!” His resolve wavered as her desperate, painful kiss met his lips. Her energy was rampaging through her body, her anguish and despair flooding into him. Jagged pulses cracked from her body, rippling the air around them. His hand came up to stop her; he was exhausted. Carrying the rebellion, his own pain, and now she was just using him. But something stirred deep inside him, fueling his own desire, ignited in response to her fervent need. His body shuddered, her energy forcing its way into his body, flooding him with pain and tearing through him.
He felt her hands on his trousers and he shook his head, but she wasn’t listening. He snarled under his breath, his resolve finally crumbling as he forced his energy over hers as she sank onto him. His Rot flooded through her, and instead of her energy roaring to meet his, he felt her slowly unravelling in his hands. Her body started going slack against him as his Rot climbed her skin like ivy, a tidal wave of splintered memories and feelings streaming through him.
He felt his mutation wrap around her core like a tightening fist, threatening to give in to her cold, desperate resignation curling at the edge of her energy. His spine went rigid with realisation, his hands gripping her hips tight enough to bruise, his vision going white around the edges. “Myth. Stop.” His voice was raw.
Her arms tightened around his neck like a brace, her forehead hot against his jaw. She wasn’t hearing him, or she didn’t care. Her energy flickered wildly, spewing cold and fiery foreign energies into his body. Residuals of other people, other energy, other thoughts splintered through his body alongside her thoughts and pain. His bloodstream screamed through his body, his heart threatening to burst as the violent energy detonated under his skin.
She was trying to kill herself through his mutation. The thought screamed through him like a jagged knife. He tore his energy back, his nerves screaming in pain. “No!” he rasped, pulling her up by her chin and looking into her broken eyes. “You don’t get to use me to kill yourself.”
Her breathing stuttered, her lips parted like she wanted to deny it, her face streaked with tears. Anger and grief hit him as she leaned forward, her body trembling against his, finally letting her mutation rush in, mending and healing her.
She was crying harder now, her energy rampaging out of control, flooding into him. He felt his body lock up, fighting to keep from being consumed by her. A violent surge of black and gold tore through him, his ribs cracking under the pressure. Fear trickled down his spine as he heard the energy suppressors kick on, remembering the last time her energy ran rampant. He pulled her unbridled energy into his body, fire searing through his veins, making his vision flash black.
He fought to contain the unbridled energies surging through his core, his heart stuttering painfully as her mutation crawled along his skin like jagged gold threads. His skin sizzled as the emergency energy suppressants flashed on, quickly drenching their bodies, the stabilising mist little more than stinging rain against her energy.
He wanted her to stop, but his body was paralysed by the avalanche of repressed emotions finally breaking free. She cried out against him, her body burning against his. His ears felt like they were underwater as he felt his mutation overload his senses, a cold numbness creeping up his fingertips. His arms slowly went numb as she stilled in his lap, her energy finally subsiding.
“Vexal…” she choked between sobs. “I’m sorry… I shouldn’t have…”
He forced his head to look down, his vision blurring slightly before focusing sharply on the Rot crawling over his skin in thick black swirls, settling into his forearms anchored by scorches of gold. He blinked hard, the storm of energy in him burning every nerve, every feeling, every painful thought. Numbing him. The grief, the panic, the begging, the pain, all burning away in an inferno of mutative energy.
He exhaled shakily, looking at Myth again. She was looking at him with a concerned expression, and he brushed his fingers along her temple, gently tucking her hair behind her ear. He kissed her forehead gently, moving her carefully, easing her back onto the bench.
He stood up slowly, fixing his trousers and walking to the door. He turned to look at her, a guilty look across her features as she clearly tried to find her words.
“Vexal… I—” she started.
“Goodbye, Myth.” The door closed behind him silently, and he swiftly made his way back out into the streets. He pulled his hood up, waiting for something to hit him. Guilt, rage, regret?
Instead, he felt nothing, the cold numbness creeping through his mind like a soothing hand. The bitter air visibly pulsed off the Outer Pulse down into the Verge, breathing around him as he walked along the root-twisted walkways with his eyes unfocused.
As he stepped back into the shadows of his transport, he immediately tapped his comtech. “Lyra,” he said, sitting down stiffly.
The line immediately crackled alive. “I’m in the west bay. Is Myth—”
“I am going to Forgedeep,” he said as he took off. “Ember Crown, the Dustmarkets.”
The line was quiet for a long breath. “What’s there?”
“One of Fane’s main augmentation sites. He has had a location there for cycle-years, but it has picked up production. Dregs being turned into walking flashcracks in exchange for complete loyalty.” His eyes narrowed forward, his thoughts finally sharpening.
“Will Fane be there?” Her voice was hesitant, but he felt no need to lie.
“I don’t know. Mobilise everyone. Your agency needs to be on the front lines to pull civilians out.”
Korric’s deep voice crackled through the line. “We have three quake teams, a few anchors, and an Echelon. We can be in and out before the T.E.B. can sufficiently mobilise.”
“Why now, Vexal?” Lyra asked suddenly.
Vexal didn’t answer her question. “I am going to be leading the assault. Give me a wide berth. I will be taking out the heavy hitters, and if Fane is there, I want you all to retreat. Make sure the AI is there to control the narrative. Do you understand?”
“Understood.” Their voices came in sync before the line crackled dead. The cabin was silent again, and the vibration of the transport’s engine pressed against his skull. He leaned back, his head tipped against the seat, his eyes half-closed as he wove carelessly in and out of traffic. His hands moved as if they had a mind of their own, veined in black and gold to his mid-forearm, where he expected to feel pain. But he still felt nothing but numbness. A numbness that seeped through his veins, his body, his mind.
The transport shuddered as it broke into the upper lanes over Forgedeep, heat rolling along the hull as the industrial vents spouted jagged streaks of red magma across the sky. The Ember Crown sat nestled on the volcanic range, a blistering ring of metal and stone high above the civilian and Null sectors that resided in the deep canyons below. Canopy nets stretched across the canyon gaps, separating and protecting the various sectors from the brutal atmosphere.
The Dustmarkets were so far down they glowed faintly like a crack in the planet's surface, and the closer Vexal cut his transport, the thinner the traffic became. He felt adrenaline steadily flood his veins as he kicked the descent thrusters on, standing as he dropped through the canopy gaps slowly. His eyes scanned the dormant lava tubes as the sky darkened far above him, the canopies effectively blocking out the light. His world became bathed in an orange glow as he passed housing units and stray Ashdogs; a few curious eyes turned in his direction, but most took no notice. He finally found an empty tube and docked his transport into the dark, the Dustmarket only a short drop below him.
He exited slowly, the heat slamming into him like a wall as he used his Rot to cushion his landing. The smell of sharp metal followed, then sweat, food, overheated plastic, and the constant undertones of something burning. The sounds rose up around him in a symphony of market noise and violence.
The heat-dispersing canopy nets criss-crossed overhead, their black carbon fibres glowing along the edges slightly from absorbing an astronomical amount of UV rays. Under them, rows of stalls fought for space, welded and patched onto whatever surface would hold. The inhabitants here had carved massive homes, stores, roads, and monuments into the sides of the obsidian-rich canyons. As he moved through the people, not focusing on anyone in particular, his eyes scanned the colossal tubes, looking for the familiar crest of red, black, and white.
He made his way to the centre of the market and opened his comtech, heading towards Lyra’s ping. He felt a shoulder brush against him and flinched away, noticing that a few eyes had begun to look his way, words failing mid-haggle as they recognised him.
He spotted the Halflight transports cutting through the nets from above, his eyes turning upwards to the big, blocky rebellion stripes, the heat from their engines rushing through the canyon. Six military-grade transports adjusted to docking ledges, the doors punching open as ramps slammed down. Heavily geared Dregs poured out, the bright red, white, and black insignia of the rebellion visible on their shoulders and chestplates.
Lyra led the stream, her face pinched, her lips in a tight line. Hundreds of operatives spilled down from the ledges in organised streams, some dropping lines into the lowest tiers while others took the stairways.
He didn’t greet their salute and started issuing orders. “Evacuate all civilians. Ensure transport passage to any place in Miridan Hollow. Broadcast everything.”
A fleet of transports grounded along the further wall behind them, dust and ash flying wild. Korric leapt from the lead transport, his heavy gauntlets thrumming with energy.
“I want you,” Vexal didn’t skip a beat, “to clean out the upper levels. Anyone that swears allegiance to Fane. We take no prisoners.”
Korric nodded with a grunt, the platoon of thick, armoured Dregs following him, their weapons glowing with heavy energy as they charged up for their first assault.
His comtech flickered to life, the Ash King’s face coming into view. “Do you wish to broadcast to all of Titan?” came his oily voice.
Vexal nodded, his face impassive.
“Understood.” The feed blinked off. Vexal took off his comtech, looking at it for a moment in his hand, before tossing it aside.
He felt his mutation roar to life as he walked to the obsidian edge, closing his eyes before dropping deep into the black market. The canopy nets hissed softly as they bled away underneath him, light filtering down on the hiding, augmented Dregs scrambling for cover.
He let his field expand, brushing against the ancient stone walls of the planet, a slow expansion that turned everything around them to ash. Dregs attempted to square off with him, charging him with their Fane-given gifts. He wanted to hate them, loathe them, pity them.
But he felt nothing as the corrosion climbed his boots and pulsed out in large arcs, ripping the screams and flesh from everything it touched. He felt blood splash across his face as the sky peeked through overhead, the brilliant red mixing with the ashy blood. He started running, feeling everything and nothing as his energy rampaged against the tide of attacking Dregs.
Overhead, he heard Lyra’s teams moving quickly, the tri-colour uniforms moving in and out of the shadows quickly and efficiently, a flood of transports racing away in the sky. As the stream of white slowed, he moved faster, his eyes barely registering the Dregs turning into ash under his fingertips. Their mutations didn’t stand a chance; even with Fane’s augmentations, they blurred together in waves of ash, voiceless whispers in the wind.
Soon the Dregs were screaming in terror, no longer trying to attack, but fleeing deeper and deeper into the tunnels. He stopped at the end of the canyon, faced with an imposing wall of obsidian, peppered with hundreds of hollowed-out tubes and cave systems. The wall reached up to the surface of Titan, the red sky screaming with magma above him. He had found it.
He heard screaming and scrambling echoing from deep inside. Hundreds of Dregs had escaped inside, but the only way out of these tunnels was in, or through a volcanic vent. Overwhelming power vibrated through his bones, exploding out of his body in a massive, arcing vortex.
It ripped up the wall in a terrible storm of black and pain. He heard a broadcast going loudly above him, a few news drones fighting to stay aloft in the heavy tide of ash. His mutation sliced through the tunnels, the entire structure collapsing into feathery light ash in a slow avalanche of black. Screams radiated through the air before cutting short, the last of the Dregs consumed by his mutation. The storm around him increased, finally reaching bedrock, leaving nothing but a spacious field of ash.
As the dust and ash settled around him, he turned to see all of the Rebellion's responders standing in formation, watching in awe. Fane had clearly not been inside, but Vexal knew it was only a matter of time before they faced retaliation, so they would take out as many of Fane’s footholds as they could, as fast as they could. They would leave nothing for him to fall back on, and force Fane Dryst to fight his own battles.


























Chapter 42
Helios Caen King sat very still as the world spun around him in a whirlwind of gold and crystal. The imposing atrium of the House of King glowed in layered shades of gold and winter-white as inlaid veins of sunstone ran through the vaulted ceiling, bending into a polished halo.
Snow drifted lazily beyond the wall of windows, but inside, hundreds of candles burned in tiered crystal cages, their flames flickering gently in the waning light. A string quartet of Luminal musicians played from an elevated platform behind him, their instruments wired with light-threads that hummed softly through the air.
His uniform was traded for a white-and-gold formal resonance suit, fitted regally, with golden tassels glinting along his back in the ambient light. His cape was stark white, the House of King emblem stamped into the silken resonance cloth. He wore his family rings like his father, and large gold cuffs sat heavy on his arms.
The conversations around him drifted across his skin; a familiar repetition of arguments echoed through the hall. The House of Solaris was there, visiting before the Cryothorne Winter Solstice began, enjoying a private festival for themselves before the sectors were allowed to celebrate.
“Cryothorne will make a spectacle as always,” his father, Lord Cassien King, snorted loudly, his heavy hand, encased in massive golden rings, hitting the marble table, his eyes flashing brightly. “A week of parades and ice sculptures while what? While Fane has his way with the planet?”
“Our people, as well as yours, like their theatrics,” Lady Isalya Solaris replied, her tone cold, but her frosty-blue cheeks tinged pink in the candlelight. “And it keeps morale high. You cannot hold a city in permanent austerity and expect blind loyalty. You have to give the people something.”
Helios resisted flinching at her words; morale would be damaged beyond repair if they lost the whole of Titan while they slept. He took a long drink out of his goblet, the crystal a welcome cool against his lips.
“Well then, Cryothorne will bankrupt itself on pageantry, gaudy displays, and frivolities,” his father stated, swirling his Aurelian nectar in a glass carved from a single sun-diamond. His ceremonial breastplate had been polished to a blinding finish, the filigree and edges scattering light in aggressive shards across the room.
“It is not the festival itself that is the problem,” Lady Solaris said.
“Honestly,” Eira chimed in, walking over to the table from chatting with one of his other cousins. “It’s a strain on resources. Cryothorne is already stretching its power grid to keep the outer rings moving. If Forgedeep could keep supplies moving…” She left off in a loud, pointed voice, obviously aimed at Darius across the room.
Darius turned from where he was talking to Nyx and Helios’s twin cousins to throw her a double finger.
Soft laughter danced through the room, the comment obviously aimed at Darius’s very noticeably absent grandfather, General Valkyrie.
“Will you not be putting on a show this cycle then, Cassien?” Lady Solaris asked coyly, running a finger across his father's glass, a small streak of frost spidering down the rim and frosting the drink.
“Well of course!” he boomed with a hearty laugh. “As you said, we have to reassure the Null districts!”
Laughter rippled through the room again, cheer and curiosity flitting through the air. A full House of King appearance anywhere was turned into a sanctified spectacle; this surely would be no different.
“And we have a new Apex-class Quasar to thank Sir Providence for as well!” Helix King, Helios’s sandy-haired younger brother, quipped.
“Anomaly-class,” Cassien corrected, his lip curling, his eyes glancing over to where Nyx stood laughing with Darius and one of the cousins of the House of Solaris, blissfully unaware of the turn of topic.
“Nevertheless,” Isalya interjected coolly. “We are grateful for all the blessings that Titan bestows upon us.”
Murmurs echoed around the atrium, soft and sharp. “Blessings,” they murmured in unison.
“Helios,” Lady Solaris said, turning her attention back to him with a small, flirtatious smile. “I do hope you will be joining us in Cryothorne? Or does Mereen have you somewhere else?”
“As if they don’t have any better use for him,” Cassien snorted, “than parading you around your frozen palace!”
“It would be my pleasure,” Helios answered with a diplomatic smile in her direction, noting how her eyelashes lowered just a fraction.
His father loudly poured another drink, raising the thin flute in a toast. “Well then! My son has decided the House of King and the House of Solaris will both be shining brightly side by side this coming cycle! Sir Providence be blessed!”
Helix rolled his eyes, loudly muttering about how Providence played favourites amidst the cheers. Eira flicked his shoulder gently, frost crackling briefly over his sleeve before she smoothed it away with a coy grin. Helios frowned.
Someone at the far end of the table started a new argument, and Helios let the noise blur away. He felt a faint pressure build at the base of his skull and he looked around, wondering whose energy signature was pulsing on the verge of control.
Helios tried to relax, but the thrum of energy under his skin kept him on edge. Finally he set his goblet down, the decisive note of crystal against marble cutting through the noise. “Excuse me.”
He offered a small nod to the table, dismissing himself and striding out past the quartet, the music fading behind him abruptly as he absent-mindedly moved through the corridors, his mind distracted.
His study was dimmer than the atrium, the single gold-and-crystal chandelier above tinkling gently in the cool air. The old-world Cappelletti Royal writing desk and furniture were bathed in golden rays from the wall of crystal overlooking the tiered spires of Helion Prime cutting into the waning light.
His eyes flickered over the stack of datapads that sat on his desk, edges still aligned with military precision with the terminal at the centre. He closed his eyes for a moment, savouring the cool, familiar atmosphere as he rolled his shoulders back.
A soft rustle of fabric sounded behind him, and the faint click of his office door being locked brought him back.
He opened his eyes and turned, seeing Isalya had let herself in, crossing the room in a white-and-blue flurry of frost, stretching out on the low couch. He walked over and sat on the low table in front of her, eyeing the frost gently curling through the air. Her bright-blue eyes darkened as she tipped her head back, looking up at him with half-closed eyes.
“You left me alone with your father and I had barely finished one drink,” she smirked in a low voice.
“We call that ‘abandoning ship’ where I’m from,” he lowered his face towards hers with a smirk.
“Do you know how many times he said ‘glass the Verge’ tonight? Mhm?” She flopped back with a dramatic laugh, her blue skin almost iridescent in the light.
“He’s consistent! What can I say?” Helios laughed, spreading his arms wide and unbuckling his cape in a flourish.
“I think he just likes to hear himself talk,” she countered with a snort. Frost curled lazily up her sapphire ankle, her bare calves stretching over the back of the white sofa. Her dress was a slash of white against her cerulean skin. “If he keeps harping on about Cryothorne’s ‘frivolity,’ I am going to freeze his tongue to his teeth and claim it was an energy flare.”
“You will cause an incident, again,” Helios murmured.
“Less of an incident than you having the newest budding Quasar under your roof,” she quipped back.
His jaw tightened and he leaned back. “Nyx is under control.”
“So I’ve heard,” she replied flippantly. “An anomaly on a barely existent leash sounds much more generous. Do you trust her?”
“She’s not my partner and not mine to trust. Voxx has—”
“Quasars are not notorious for respecting reputation, let alone each other.” She ran a frost-blue nail down his arm, leaving a trail of ice.
“The same thing could be said for your daughter.” His voice was smug as a smile crept across his face.
That earned him a sharp smile. “Yes, which is why she is on a very, very short leash. The AAN needs her as the symbol of Cryothorne, and people need their symbols.”
“The people need heat and food, and structures that don’t collapse,” he said pointedly.
“And grids nationwide that don’t blow every time someone with a powerful mutation sneezes!” Isalya added dryly. “Yes. I am aware.”
She shifted up, sitting so she could look him squarely in the face, the motion dragging her dress high along one thigh. Frost crystallised at the top of the diamond-encrusted silken slit. “Helion keeps telling us to hold out for the Foundation Track, hold out for enough Lummies to get it together enough to fix the Catalyst Wells, hold the Null Zone. Meanwhile,” she huffed, his eyes dragging momentarily to the ice curling down her neck, “Fane is crawling around my rings like he owns them!” She caught sight of his wandering eyes and snapped her thin, blue fingers at him.
He sighed and dragged his eyes slowly over her body back to her face. “There is nothing I myself can do for you about any of that at this exact moment. I’m coming to the festival and so is the rest of the Apex Corps. That is the best assurance I can give you that everything will be fine.” He slid to the edge of the table, his knees brushing the couch. Up close, the blue iridescence under her skin was even more eye-catching, a sharp contrast to her silvery-white hair. He held out his hand, and she rested her face against his palm. Her hand came up and he grabbed her wrist, his grip like iron pulling her up just enough to toss her over his lap as he settled into the cushions.
“About time,” she murmured, her lips finding his, an icy storm slamming through him.
His hands found the back clasp of her gown, an intricate frost-threaded fabric, and ripped it away from her body. She retaliated in kind, her sub-zero hands tearing at the front of his formal wear, the expensive golden buttons flying open from her barely-contained power.
He drove into her, her icy body wrapping around him, the temperature dropping so low it made his breath catch. His Solar Resonance flared on reflex, the white-hot heat rising as each thrust sent a shockwave of energy through her body.
Their bodies tangled together in a whirlwind of power, the couch beneath them groaning, the ancient frame creaking in protest as the air crackled with static. As their minds began to parallel, her power unleashed a wave of frozen calm through his body, her mutation a welcome relief.
As the pressure in his body peaked, their comtechs both blared to light and they both jerked apart, his heartbeat pulsing through his ears as the screen blackened and a jagged crown of black flared across a field of red. Underneath, clean white text read:


THE ASH BLIGHT REBELLION’S SUCCESSFUL EFFORT OF PURGING THE FORGEDEEP DUSTMARKETS OF FANE DRYST. THE ONLY EMERGENCY RESPONSE TEAM ON SITE WAS THE HALFLIGHT HERO AGENCY. THANK YOU TO THEM AND VEXAL OPHEDIUS. THE DUSTMARKETS REPORTED ZERO CASUALTIES AND ALL CIVILIANS RETURNED TO DUTY.


He felt Isalya’s fingers dig into his skin, ice flooding his veins as she seethed, “Oh, for fuck’s sake.”
The black dropped, replaced by the Dustmarkets view from a high vantage point, heat and dust causing the frame to flicker. The camera panned, tracking a Halflight squad as their red, white, and black uniforms cut crisp formations against the ash.
He felt his hands dig into her icy hips as he saw civilians filing out of the canyon, transports leaving in a perfect single-file line while a dark-clad Vexal Ophedius blazed a trail of ash through the mutated Dregs, leaving nothing behind. A clean and perfectly efficient response. One Apex Dreg did in a minute what the Corps had been failing to do for cycles.
“Treason,” Isalya seethed, her voice tight. The feed ended with an Ash King AI broadcast channel. She leapt off his lap in a flurry of ice as he righted himself. His energy, chaotic and wild, roared through him, his thoughts honed and narrow. Finding his cloak, he secured it before triggering the liquid breathing mask. He heard her rushing out of the room behind him as he flung the balcony doors open, the crystal booming against the metal walls. With one step he walked into the night sky, the freezing air rushing past him as his energy rushed around him, light exploding out of his body.
He saw Darius’s white transport jetting to the AAN tower, and he exploded ahead. The wind roared past his ears as his mind whirled, the lingering ice of Isalya’s hands pricking his skin. He cut harder through the air, the face of Ophedius, his half-cyborg face burning a harsh image in his mind, the tendrils of dark Rot and ash curling around everything in his way.
Zero casualties were almost unheard of; even for him that was a rare accomplishment. Even if they had just got lucky, the AAN would want a formal reaction.
He broke the cloud layer as the Ascension Spire speared up to meet him, the polished obsidian glass twinkling in the evening light. He didn’t bother with the transport bay as he noted Darius and Nyx racing along the arced pathway from the bay to the briefing room. Instead, he dropped into a vertical dive, slicing through the open roof and slamming onto the dais in a flash of white and ice. The impact sent a tremor through the deck as the rest of the Apex Corps flooded the room in a clamour of voices.
Mereen rushed forward, her clothes festive, but her expression furious. “General.” Her voice cracked across the room like a whip, dozens of conversations stopping as she strode onto the dais. The last wisps of frost rolled off him as he stepped to the head of the room with Voxx and Mereen.
She stabbed an angry, gloved finger at the central terminal, the air above it igniting into a frozen frame from that ratty AI’s broadcast.
The Dustmarkets in all their grimy glory, a clear view of Ophedius’s face outlined with the damning white text outlining the indisputable victory.
Zero casualties. For a hero agency that was a celebratory win, but for a Dreg hero agency?
Mereen finally spoke, her voice dangerously quiet. “There is a Winter Solstice victory parade happening in the Dustmarkets right now, in fact.”
A wave of muttered curses and disgruntled threats rippled through the group, and he felt Eira’s frost sharply as it exploded in a sheet underfoot.
“Treason!” Seren Kaelith practically yelled, his mirrored body causing the light around him to dance wildly as his body blinked in and out of sight.
“Everyone, somehow, was late to the party. While this Dreg hero agency…” Mereen seethed. “While we can scrub the servers, we cannot, in fact, scrub the entire planet's memory.”
“So let's be precise!” Voxx cut in, his voice a low thrum of barely-contained fury. “The official record should state that the AAN was on site; we, alongside a local Hero Agency, achieved a perfect civilian extraction in a zone where collateral was unacceptable. It will be a testament to how wise the AAN is for having sanctioned heroes in the first place.”
The small silence that followed was thick with energy. Lynx stepped into the light, his finger pointing accusingly at Halflight. “Terminate their hero licences.”
Nyx and Rhea both started protesting. “They saved hundreds of families!” Rhea’s voice carried over the din.
“You will make them martyrs!” Nyx’s voice followed after.
“We don’t negotiate with terrorists who wear hero badges!” Eira screamed, ice lancing through the room towards Nyx.
Darius blocked the blast easily with his gauntlet, his voice enraged. “Terrorists? Are you insane?!”
“Oh, don’t play holier-than-thou with me! You have erased a sector block for significantly less!” she spat back, Mereen holding her back by her shoulders.
Lynx leapt to the centre, his Nullify-field battling with the push of Apex energy. “Why don’t we ask your partner there then, Darius? Hm? Since she is training them. Is one of them!”
“What was their plan, Nyx?” Mereen asked pointedly.
All eyes turned to her, but her dark face was impassive. “If you’re asking if I knew this raid was coming, no, I did not,” she said, her voice flat. “I was at the King estate with Darius when this went down anyway.”
“Oh, how fortuitous,” Eira sneered, the room dropping colder, Lynx’s nullification taxed to the limits, unable to hold back the continuous overflow of mutations in the room. “The rebellion’s best spy is winning and dining, conveniently, hundreds of kilometres away. What? Too big a risk of getting caught on camera?”
Darius rounded on her. “Say that to my face, Ice Princess!” His body flamed violet-orange.
Helios almost stepped forward, but Rhea was quicker. “Accusing each other is not going to solve the problem!”
“What even is the problem?” Dr. Ekros said, his face coming out of the shadows. “This would be a perfect time to work with the responsible agency and gather more intel on Ophedius, not alienate them. Certainly not during the Winter Solstice.”
“I am afraid Varian has a point,” Mereen said heavily, giving Eira an eye before letting her go. She walked to the console, her white gloves flicking quickly through the data. “Right now the public response is at an all-time high. Not only for the Hero Agency responsible, but for the Hero Act and AAN as a whole.”
“So you expect us to just bow down to the Rebellion?!” Voxx’s voice rang out indignantly.
“No.” Mereen’s eyes glowed with pure, terrifying energy. “But we will acknowledge the incident. The problem, Varian, is the Rebellion not only getting a free PR boost, but giving the illusion we are on working terms with them.”
“We cannot have an Apex-level Dreg running around leading a successful rebellion!” Helios finally burst out, his anger reaching a boiling point. “We will not be seen as tyrants. I move that we formally reprimand the Halflight Hero Agency for failure to capture or engage the wanted criminal, Vexal Ophedius. Countered with a medal of bravery for the response.”
“I concur,” Mereen said coolly.
“Furthermore, we add new restrictions to the hero licencing, ensuring that clear sympathisers never slip through our ranks again.” His eyes narrowed on Nyx. “I, personally, will lead a brigade to target at least one of the rebellion's strongholds. Any objections?”
“No,” Mereen answered for the group, her composure returning. “And neither you nor your House will be icing the Verge. That includes you, Solaris. We will present a calm, unified front for the opening grand ceremony of the Winter Solstice. But none of you are on vacation until I have Vexal Ophedius. Dead or alive.”
Good.
























Chapter 43
The first bell tolled high above Nyx, a shiver of bronze that rolled across the Cryothorne Winter Solstice platform and died in the vast, frozen Frostline Divide. The sky above was a deep, bruised violet, cut with bright bands of an aurora, the greens and purples illuminating the crowd in a jagged silhouette of suspended ice-terraces and open transport decks. Frost-rimed orange banners snapped in the thin air, their blue stitched sigils flashing with sapphires. Snow fell around her ears in gentle flakes down to the crystal flow that hissed gently with the heat.
She pulled her light-blue and orange coat tighter around her, the thin fabric a meagre defence against the cold. She was grateful the heavy orange wool skirt covered her legs, Darius’s molten family crest stitched in red around the hem. Darius stood next to her, his formal armour a mirror to her outfit. His orange cape fluttered behind him gently in the wind, his hair smouldering in the cold. His uniform was light blue trimmed with black, matching his gauntlets and boots.
They were spaced alongside the railing with her at the end, then Darius, Eira, Seren, Lynx, Voxx, Helios, Rhea, and the rest of the operatives, cadets, and officers. The High Council stood behind them, their speeches over, words still echoing through the frozen land.
Sir Providence’s bright effigy loomed sapphire and gold in front of the six Providence Rings of Cryothorne. They were beautiful  skeletal arcs of crystalline glory threaded around the mountain's ice shelf like the eye of a sleeping giant; dormant, they were nearly invisible in the sky.
A ripple of anticipation caught her attention, and the vendors stilled as the whine of distant music faded into silence. Children were hoisted onto shoulders, and a sea of blue faces turned to the darkening sky.
General King slowly rose into the frosty sky, his uniform full formal AAN and Cryothorne battle regalia: white-and-gold body armour lined with bright flashes of orange garnet gemstones. His light-blue cape, stamped with the King House sigil in gold, fluttered majestically in the air behind him as he drew his energy together.
Nyx felt the low thrum beneath her boots, and she felt a trickle of dread down her back that she quickly stamped down. The air tightened around her, pulling taut like a drumskin as the sliver of sunlight seemed to curdle as it melted away into the rings and gas steadily eclipsing it.
As the eclipse reached totality, the General’s chest began to glow white, quickly engulfing his body in a blinding light of pure energy. He raised his hands as he approached the closest ring, the heat from his body dispersing the clouds, leaving the rings in plain view.
The voice of the High Chancellor rang through the speakers overhead:


“Titan, we welcome the two hundred and ninety-sixth annual eclipse! May Sir Providence shine good luck upon you!”


A solid beam of energy exploded upward from King’s body, lighting up the sky almost as bright as day. The crystal arc erupted in a flash of light and ice as the energy flashed from one ring to the next. A low hum sounded, and she felt her molars vibrate as the concentric halos began to turn, steadily gaining speed as the sharp smell of metal and ice cut through her senses. Every hair on her arms lifted in the static charge, and below them, the crowd erupted in a collective celebration. Children shrieked and families embraced as the energy slowly warmed the square, Luminals pressing their hands together in silent thanks.
King lowered himself slowly, his light subsiding steadily as the broadcast around them came to life with information about the available events.
She didn’t hear a word as they were released to “mingle,” and for the next few hours, she was a mindless prop. She stood at the AAN recruitment pavilion, smiling for flash after flash as the crowds begged for pictures and signed posters. The noises layered into a cacophony in her skull, and she tried not to focus on the delightful smells of soups and sweets heavy in the air.
“Nyx?” Darius’s voice brought her back to the present abruptly. “Are you okay?”
“I’m great!” she said brightly. “I am going to take a loop around the market, get the kids we missed, and use the lav!” She slipped away before he could answer, plunging into the current of the brightly coloured crowd.
The market terrace was a river of happy noises and colourful bodies. Strings of frozen lights glittered overhead, while ice sculptures lined the path, depicting heroes and beasts alike clashing in fierce battles.
As she walked, her senses continued to stretch taut, her eyes scanning the crowd. Echoes of the earlier conversation rang in her ears, and the Halflight broadcast scratched at the back of her mind, the way she had thought the ruse was surely up when Eira had accused her of being with the rebellion. She had no idea when Vexal had left her that he was going to potentially upstage all of their efforts to handle Fane. To make it worse, he had shown he was clearly an Apex-level threat, and the responding agency had been Lyra’s. She wondered if they had planned everything together, but she doubted it. She hadn’t even known about the House of King hosting a pre-festival event, let alone her and Darius being specifically invited to attend. Still… she chewed the inside of her lip as she stopped behind a bright orange-and-white-striped stall, the vendor’s voice booming with festive noise as he advertised his goods.
She took a slow, deliberate breath, trying to calm her nerves and the gnawing pit of energy in her stomach. On her second breath, she finally caught it, something souring the sweet smell of the frying foods. Something acrid and chemical that burned her throat and eyes immediately. Her pulse screamed through her body and she froze; surely not—
“Blessed Winter Solstice, Myth.” His voice was right at her shoulder, calm and intimate in the din of noise.
She turned her head carefully. Fane was standing frighteningly close considering someone so large was able to slip in and out of the crowd. He was dressed in traditional blue-and-orange festival clothing, a large coat of dark wool over his shoulders concealing his support gear. His black eyes honed in on her lips as snow dusted his eyelashes.
“If you so much as twitch, I will let loose a flood of acid that will eradicate this entire ice shelf,” he said lightly, the tone of his voice belying the gravity of his words. “I just wanted to have a chat.”
Her heart twisted in her chest, her mind racing frantically. “Are you demented?” she hissed.
“Why yes, thank you for noticing,” he chuckled.
The crowd flowed around them, laughter and fireworks bursting overhead, their lights flashing across the festival, highlighting the thousands of people milling in and out of stalls and amusements.
“You shouldn’t be here,” she finally said, calming her nerves.


“Oh? And miss hearing Mereen’s tight-lipped praises for Dregs in person? I think not,” he laughed outright at that. “I know the rebellion is finally gaining ground while you stand here parading around like a prized pet! Must feel great.”
She bristled, the urge to lash out with her energy and rip him to shreds rising. He was right. The damage he could do, even in a second, would be catastrophic. And not just to her, but to Cryothorne as a whole.
“I have to go,” he said, taking one step before stopping just out of her line of sight. “I actually have a warning for you. Stand down.”
She jerked her head involuntarily. “What?”
He leaned in so close she could hear his respirator, slow and persistent, pulsing through the air. “Stand. Down. You and whoever you care about. If you don’t want to get shredded by the hell that is coming, stand down.”
A group of boisterous teenagers jostled past, laughing loudly, and she whirled around, trying to confront Fane under the cover of the crowd. But he was gone. She whirled around, her eyes scanning frantically, but she saw nothing. A surge of frustration rose in her as she pushed down the street; he couldn’t have gotten far, and the fact he had even gotten into Cryothorne was a major security breach.
Her heart hammered against her ribs as she moved, shoving through the flow of bodies, her breath catching in her throat as she tried to follow his very distinct scent. But it was no use; he had disappeared.
She pushed until she reached the main promenade, the AAN pavilion coming into view with Darius standing in front. He was laughing with a group of Cryothorne workers who took turns holding his gauntlets for pictures.
“Darius.” Her voice came out sharper than she intended.
His head snapped up, his eyes locking onto her face immediately. “Hey guys, give me five.” He nodded to the group, meeting her at the corner of the booth. “What happened?” he asked quietly, his eyes focused on hers intently.
“Fane was here,” she whispered.
Every muscle in his body went rigid, and he bent his head to her ear. “Say that again?”
“Fane. Dryst. Was here,” she gritted out. “He came up behind me in the market. He threatened to let loose enough acid to erase the entire ice shelf if I tried anything.”
“How did security miss that?” Darius muttered under his breath. “Why you? What did he want?”
She swallowed hard. “He said he wanted to warn me. He said, ‘Stand down. You and anybody who I care about should stand down.’”
“That’s it? And what does that mean, exactly?” His eyes narrowed as he searched hers.
“I… I really don’t know,” she answered, defeated. “He was gone before I could ask. I searched all the way back here, but I didn’t see him or smell him again.”
“I’m alerting the system; that way all security can be on high alert since we know for a fact that he’s here,” Darius said as he tapped through his comtech, the display flashing orange against his face as he spoke in an authoritative tone:


PRIORITY ALERT: VERIFIED FANE DRYST CONTACT INSIDE FESTIVAL PERIMETER. UNKNOWN ENTRY AND EXIT POINTS, NO CASUALTIES REPORTED, MOTIVE UNKNOWN. IF SIGHTED DO NOT ENGAGE WITHOUT OFFICIAL DIRECTION.


He cut the line, and they stood there quietly. He finally sighed heavily. “Don’t wander off again,” he said as he walked back to the eagerly waiting group of fans.
“Wasn’t planning on it,” she muttered under her breath as she made her way over to a crowd of gathering fans, their pointing and whispers earning a genuine smile out of her.
The next hours blurred together as they finished fan service, patrol routes, and a perimeter sweep, every officer on standby as the Solstice’s first twelve hours drew to a close, the day finally ending.
Her nerves stayed tight, and every loud sound or scream made her jump, expecting to see Fane wreaking havoc upon the blissfully unaware civilians. But he didn’t come back.
Eventually, the rotation was complete, and the T.E.B. shuttled in to take the night watch, leaving her and Darius to walk back to the Cryothorne Apex Suites in silence. She was chilled to the bone, and not just from the icy wind that had picked up as the night progressed, or the haunting moan of Saturn's rings cutting a chaotic scene across the sky.
They took the crystal lift into the uppermost spires while Darius kept busy, issuing reports on his comtech. The luxury suites themselves were perched on a narrow ledge jutting out of the permanent ice shelf, the corridors themselves part blue ice, part rich dark wood. It was silent except for the low hum of the Rings in the distance.
Darius palmed the door open, the lock clicking behind them as she surveyed the interior. The walls were smoked crystal and rich dark wood, embellished with silver filigree and expensive artworks. The floor was a pale, polished stone, but was comfortably heated, and she quickly kicked her boots off, savouring the heat under her feet. A wide couch faced a wall display and terminal, and in the back, another room opened into a bedroom suite, a massive white bed anchoring the space.
Darius set his gear down carefully at the entrance; his face looked tired and drawn. She peeled off her clothes, carefully laying them out before settling down, since she didn’t have her uniform on hand if an emergency happened.
“You’re shaking,” Darius spoke quietly behind her.
“I’m cold,” she said lightly, even though her hands continued to shake violently.
“Myth.” The way he said her name left no room for argument.
She felt a twist in her chest as she turned to him, the quiet of the room feeling like it was going to swallow her whole.
“What?” she asked, trying to make her voice sound lively. “It’s been a long day.”
Darius had shed his armour, and his hair hung slightly damp across his eyes. “Yeah,” he agreed quietly. “It has.”
He moved across the room, stopping just short of her, his chest a hand's width from her face. She looked up at him, taking in the heavy bags around his eyes.
“Do you want to synergise?” he asked, his hand brushing her chin.


Her energy flared at the thought, a restless, fiery lurch under her skin, wanting nothing more than to fall into his arms and lose herself in his molten inferno. She squeezed her eyes closed, turning her head away as a wave of Vexal’s Rot roiled through her, her mutation still threaded with veins of his Rot, the ugliness of what she had done to him vivid in her mind.
“I don’t think that’s a good idea,” she said quietly. She focused her eyes out the wall of windows and bit back the tears threatening to form.
“What do you mean?” He drew back at her words, his hand dropping.
“My head is a mess, I stink like a trash heap, I’m exhausted. Tomorrow.” Her words hung in the air; his body went very still, but the glow around him flickered violently.
“Myth.” He looked at her for a long time, his eyes zeroing in on every feature before he let out another heavy sigh. “Okay. Let’s get ready for bed; we have patrols in eleven hours.”
She blinked. “Just like that?”
He gave her a questioning look as he moved to the shower. “Yes. Come on. I’m tired,” he laughed lightly.
She let out a slow breath, following him slowly into the shower, her mind a bundle of confusing thoughts tangled with guilt.
Her hair was still damp against the pillow as she lay curled on her side, the room dark except for the view of Saturn looming through the window, its rings knifing comets through the sky. The suite was dark, but the bed was so white it seemed to glow. Darius had his arm hooked around her waist, and she pressed her back against his chest, savouring the warmth he radiated in the otherwise freezing-cold room.
Her fingers traced the scars on his arms absent-mindedly, trying to shut out the thoughts that were screaming at her. Images of Vexal flickered through her mind, and she forced the guilt and thoughts further down as she felt his energy slowly ebb from her body.
